B. F. Skinner developed operant conditioning and the philosophy of radical behaviourism: even private events (thoughts, feelings) are behaviour governed by environmental contingencies, with no need for mental causes. Abraham Maslow is the humanistic theorist of the hierarchy of needs and self-actualisation. J. B. Watson is founder of (methodological) behaviourism (1913).

Key distinctions

TheoristTheory or labelSignature
Dollard and MillerEarly social learningDrive, cue, response, reward; Freud plus Hull
Bandura and WaltersLater social learning (social cognitive)Observational learning, Bobo doll, self-efficacy
SkinnerRadical behaviourism, operant conditioningSkinner box, schedules of reinforcement
WatsonMethodological behaviourismLittle Albert (1920)
MaslowSelf-actualisationHierarchy of needs
RotterSocial learning theory (cognitive)Locus of control

MCQ traps

  • Bobo doll is never Skinner: Skinner required the learner's own reinforcement; Bandura's point is that learning occurs without it.
  • In match-the-following, "early" vs "later" social learning is the trap: Dollard and Miller are earlier (1941/1950), Bandura and Walters later (1963).
  • Radical behaviourism belongs to Skinner, not Watson; Watson's is methodological.
  • Self-actualisation goes to Maslow (or Rogers), never to a behaviourist.
  • Do not confuse self-efficacy (Bandura) with self-esteem (global worth) or locus of control (Rotter).
  • Solve match questions by fixing the surest pair first (Skinner = radical behaviourism eliminates options a and d immediately).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q20: who is known for observational learning and the Bobo doll experiment → Albert Bandura, whose 1961 to 1965 studies showed children imitate an aggressive model without direct reinforcement.
  • Q7: match Dollard and Miller, Skinner, Bandura and Walters, Maslow to theories → A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2 (early social learning, radical behaviourism, later social learning, self-actualisation), because Dollard and Miller's Hullian version preceded Bandura's.
34Hypnosis
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q39, Q55

From scratch

Hypnosis is a procedure in which a hypnotist suggests changes in sensation, perception, thought or behaviour, and a participant responds. Its chief characteristic is increased suggestibility: heightened responsiveness to suggestion together with narrowed, focused attention and reduced critical judgement. Induction usually involves relaxation and focusing on one stimulus, so arousal goes down, not up. EEG during hypnosis resembles relaxed wakefulness, not sleep.

History in order: Franz Anton Mesmer (1770s, Vienna and Paris) claimed cures through "animal magnetism"; a 1784 French commission (including Benjamin Franklin) attributed the effects to imagination. James Braid, a Scottish surgeon, coined "hypnosis" (from Greek hypnos, sleep) in 1843 and recast it as focused attention. Jean-Martin Charcot at the Salpetriere in Paris showed in dramatic demonstrations that hysterical symptoms (paralysis, anaesthesia, loss of feeling in limbs, fainting) could be produced and removed by hypnosis and suggestion; he believed only hysterics could be hypnotised. The rival Nancy school (Liebeault and Bernheim) argued hypnosis is simply suggestion and normal people are hypnotisable, and Bernheim won that debate. Freud studied with Charcot (1885 to 1886), used hypnosis with Breuer (Studies on Hysteria, 1895, Anna O. and the cathartic method), then abandoned it for free association. Pierre Janet developed dissociation theory from hypnotic work.

Two modern theories. Neodissociation theory (Ernest Hilgard, 1970s): consciousness splits; a "hidden observer" remains aware of pain while the hypnotised person reports none (ice-water experiments). Social-cognitive or role theory (Theodore Sarbin, Nicholas Spanos, Barber, Kirsch): hypnotised people are enacting the role of a hypnotised person, driven by expectations and motivation, not an altered state. Hypnotisability is a stable trait measured by the Stanford Hypnotic Susceptibility Scale (Weitzenhoffer and Hilgard) and the Harvard Group Scale; roughly 10 to 15 percent are highly hypnotisable.

What hypnosis can and cannot do: effective for pain relief (hypnoanalgesia), as an adjunct in anxiety, IBS and habit control; produces posthypnotic suggestion and posthypnotic amnesia. It does not reliably enhance memory accuracy (it increases confidence and false memories, hence hypnotically refreshed testimony is suspect), does not give superhuman strength, and generally cannot make people act against their values. Example: told "your arm is becoming light and will float up", a highly hypnotisable person's arm rises and feels involuntary.

Key distinctions

PairDistinction
Mesmer vs BraidMesmer: animal magnetism (1770s). Braid: coined "hypnosis" (1843).
Charcot vs Nancy schoolCharcot: hypnotisability is a sign of hysteria. Liebeault and Bernheim: it is normal suggestibility.
Hilgard vs SpanosHilgard: neodissociation, hidden observer. Spanos/Sarbin: social-cognitive role enactment.
Hypnosis vs sleepHypnosis: awake, alpha-like EEG, responsive. Sleep: unresponsive, sleep stages.
Hypnosis vs meditationHypnosis: responding to someone's suggestions. Meditation: self-regulated attention, no suggestion needed.

MCQ traps

  • "Heightened arousal" is wrong: induction lowers arousal; the defining feature is suggestibility.
  • "Enhanced memory" or "enhanced creativity" sound appealing but are not defining; hypnotic recall is less reliable.
  • For Charcot, "hypnosis" alone and "suggestion" alone are both partial; when "both" is offered, pick it.
  • The term hypnosis is Braid's, not Mesmer's; "mesmerism" is Mesmer's.
  • Freud dropped hypnosis for free association; do not list hypnosis as a core psychoanalytic technique.
  • Hidden observer = Hilgard, not Charcot or Janet.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q39: chief characteristic of hypnosis → Increased suggestibility, the defining hallmark; arousal actually falls and creativity or self-esteem are not core.
  • Q55: Charcot showed hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in limbs or fainting could be produced by → Both hypnosis and suggestion, since his Salpetriere demonstrations induced and removed symptoms with both.
35Double bind (Bateson) and countertransference
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q98, Q99

From scratch

Double bind: introduced by Gregory Bateson with Don Jackson, Jay Haley and John Weakland in "Toward a theory of schizophrenia" (1956), from the Palo Alto (later Mental Research Institute) group. A double bind is a communication in which a person, usually a child, receives two contradictory messages at different levels from a significant other, typically a verbal message contradicted by a non-verbal one, while a third, implicit injunction forbids commenting on the contradiction or leaving the field. Whatever the child does is wrong. Classic example: a mother says "Come and give me a hug, I love you", then stiffens and pulls away when the child approaches; if the child withdraws she says "Don't you love your mother?" Bateson proposed that repeated exposure could contribute to schizophrenia. The causal claim is no longer accepted (schizophrenia is understood through the stress-vulnerability model with strong genetic loading), but the concept shaped family systems and communication therapy.

Related family-theory concepts for schizophrenia, often put together in MCQs: schizophrenogenic mother (Frieda Fromm-Reichmann, 1948: cold, domineering, rejecting), marital schism and marital skew (Theodore Lidz), pseudomutuality and pseudohostility (Lyman Wynne), and expressed emotion (George Brown, Vaughn and Leff: criticism, hostility, emotional overinvolvement), which predicts relapse, not onset, and is the one with strong empirical support.

Transference (Freud): the patient unconsciously redirects feelings from earlier significant figures (usually parents) onto the therapist. Positive transference = affection, idealisation; negative transference = hostility, distrust. Analysis of transference is central to psychoanalysis. Countertransference (Freud, 1910): the therapist's emotional reactions to the patient, originally from the therapist's own unresolved conflicts. Freud saw it as an obstacle to be overcome by the analyst's own analysis; later writers (Paula Heimann, 1950; Racker; Winnicott's "Hate in the countertransference", 1949) treated it as a useful clue to the patient's inner world. It can be positive countertransference (over-liking, rescuing) or negative countertransference (irritation, boredom, dislike, hostility toward the patient).

The key to all four transference terms: first ask whose feelings (patient = transference, therapist = countertransference), then ask which direction (warm = positive, hostile = negative). Example: a therapist feels impatient and cold toward a demanding client who reminds him of his critical father: negative countertransference.

Key distinctions

TermWhose feelingsValence
Positive transferencePatient toward therapistWarm, idealising
Negative transferencePatient toward therapistHostile
Positive countertransferenceTherapist toward patientWarm, over-involved
Negative countertransferenceTherapist toward patientHostile, rejecting
  • Double bind (Bateson) vs schizophrenogenic mother (Fromm-Reichmann) vs marital skew/schism (Lidz) vs pseudomutuality (Wynne) vs expressed emotion (Brown).
  • Double bind vs mixed message or ordinary faulty communication: the double bind needs the contradiction, a significant relationship, and the impossibility of escape or comment.

MCQ traps

  • "Faulty communication" is a vague umbrella; when the stem describes two simultaneous conflicting messages, the technical term is double bind.
  • Bateson is the double-bind name, not Lidz or Wynne; do not give the schizophrenogenic mother to Bateson.
  • Expressed emotion predicts relapse, not causation of schizophrenia.
  • "Negative transference" tempts when you see "negative feelings", but the feelings are the therapist's, so it must be countertransference.
  • Plain "countertransference" is right but less precise; when the stem specifies negative feelings and "negative countertransference" is offered, the specific option wins.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q98: one parent conveys two conflicting messages to the child at the same time → Double bind communication, Bateson and colleagues' 1956 term for a no-win contradiction across message levels.
  • Q99: therapist expresses his negative feelings towards patients → Negative counter transference, because the feelings are the therapist's (counter) and hostile (negative); negative transference would be the patient's.
36Exposure and response prevention (ERP) and its principle
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q100

From scratch

Exposure and response prevention (ERP, also ExRP) is the first-line psychological treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder. It was pioneered by Victor Meyer (1966, Middlesex Hospital, London, "apotrepic therapy") and developed by Isaac Marks, Stanley Rachman and especially Edna Foa (University of Pennsylvania). Its rationale comes from Mowrer's two-factor theory (1947, 1960): fear of a stimulus is acquired by classical conditioning and maintained by operant (negative) reinforcement, because escape or avoidance reduces anxiety. In OCD, obsessions raise anxiety and compulsions (washing, checking, mental rituals) bring quick relief, which negatively reinforces the compulsion and prevents the person from ever learning that the feared outcome would not occur.

ERP has two parts. Exposure: systematic, prolonged contact with the feared stimulus, in vivo or imaginal (touching a "contaminated" doorknob, leaving the stove unchecked). Response prevention: the client refrains from the ritual and from subtle neutralising or reassurance seeking. Exposure is usually graded up a hierarchy using SUDS (subjective units of distress, Wolpe's 0 to 100 scale). Example: a patient with contamination fears touches a bathroom floor, does not wash for an hour, and records SUDS falling from 85 to 30 over the session and lower again across sessions.

The principle behind it is habituation and extinction. Habituation is the decrease in response to a repeated or prolonged stimulus: anxiety, if not escaped, rises, peaks and then declines on its own (within-session habituation), and starts lower each session (between-session habituation). Extinction is the weakening of a conditioned response when the CS occurs without the UCS (respondent extinction), and the weakening of the ritual when it no longer produces relief (operant extinction). Foa and Kozak's emotional processing theory (1986) adds that the fear structure must be activated and then corrected with disconfirming information. The newer inhibitory learning model (Michelle Craske, 2008, 2014) argues that extinction creates a new "safe" association that competes with the old one, with expectancy violation as the key; this is why relapse can happen through spontaneous recovery, renewal and reinstatement.

Contrast with systematic desensitisation (Joseph Wolpe, 1958), based on reciprocal inhibition or counterconditioning: a hierarchy is climbed while the client is deeply relaxed (Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation), so a response incompatible with anxiety replaces it. ERP uses no competing response; the anxiety is allowed to run its course. Flooding (in vivo, maximal exposure from the start) and implosion (Stampfl, imaginal and exaggerated, with psychodynamic themes) are also extinction-based exposure methods.

Key distinctions

TechniqueFounderUnderlying principle
ERPMeyer (1966), FoaHabituation and extinction
Systematic desensitisationWolpe (1958)Reciprocal inhibition / counterconditioning
FloodingExposure therapistsExtinction (in vivo, intense)
Implosive therapyStampfl (1967)Extinction (imaginal, exaggerated)
Aversion therapyvariousClassical conditioning (pairing with an aversive UCS)
  • Habituation (non-associative, response to a stimulus wanes with exposure) vs extinction (associative, a learned CS-UCS or response-reinforcer link weakens).
  • Graded exposure is a method of pacing, not a learning principle.

MCQ traps

  • Reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's principle for systematic desensitisation; it is the most common wrong answer for ERP.
  • Counterconditioning needs a competing response (relaxation, eating); ERP has none.
  • Graded exposure looks right because ERP often is graded, but the question asks for the principle, not the procedure.
  • Do not stop at "exposure" alone; without response prevention the ritual keeps being negatively reinforced and treatment fails.
  • ERP is behavioural; pharmacological first line for OCD is SSRIs (clomipramine the TCA). Do not mix up the two when asked which is "psychological first line".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q100: ERP is based on the behaviour therapy principle of → Habituation and extinction, because anxiety is allowed to fall naturally without the ritual, so the fear and the compulsion both lose their reinforcement; counterconditioning and reciprocal inhibition need a competing response, and graded exposure is only a technique.
37Reliability vs validity
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q75

From scratch

Every psychological test is judged on two core psychometric properties. Reliability is the consistency of a measure: if you measured the same person again, under similar conditions, would you get the same score? Validity is the accuracy or meaningfulness of a measure: does the test actually measure what it claims to measure, and do the inferences drawn from its scores hold up? The modern framework comes from classical test theory (Spearman, early 1900s), in which every observed score X = true score T + error E. Reliability is the proportion of observed-score variance that is true-score variance, so it runs from 0 to 1; most clinical tests aim for 0.80 or higher, and 0.90 for high-stakes individual decisions.

Types of reliability. (1) Test-retest: give the same test twice and correlate the scores; the result is a coefficient of stability, threatened by practice and memory effects and by real change over the interval. (2) Parallel or alternate forms: two equivalent versions given to the same people; a coefficient of equivalence. (3) Split-half: split one test into two halves (usually odd versus even items), correlate them, then correct upward with the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula, because a half-length test underestimates reliability. (4) Internal consistency: Kuder-Richardson formulas (KR-20, KR-21, Kuder and Richardson 1937) for dichotomous items, and Cronbach's alpha (Cronbach 1951) for multi-point items. (5) Inter-rater (inter-scorer) reliability: agreement between two scorers, indexed by percentage agreement, Cohen's kappa (chance-corrected) or the intraclass correlation. The standard error of measurement (SEM) translates reliability into a band of error around an individual's score: the higher the reliability, the smaller the SEM.

Types of validity. (1) Face validity: the test looks like it measures the construct to the test-taker; it is not a technical form of validity. (2) Content validity: the items adequately sample the whole domain, judged by experts (for example, an anxiety scale that covers cognitive, somatic and behavioural symptoms). (3) Criterion-related validity: scores correlate with an external criterion, either concurrent (criterion measured at the same time, such as a new depression scale against a psychiatrist's diagnosis today) or predictive (criterion measured later, such as an entrance test against final-year marks). (4) Construct validity (Cronbach and Meehl 1955): the test behaves as theory predicts; evidence includes convergent validity (high correlation with measures of the same construct) and discriminant/divergent validity (low correlation with unrelated constructs), formalised in Campbell and Fiske's (1959) multitrait-multimethod matrix. Messick (1989) argued all validity is ultimately construct validity.

The relationship. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity. An unreliable test cannot be valid, because random error cannot correlate with anything; the validity coefficient can never exceed the square root of the reliability coefficient. But a test can be perfectly reliable and still invalid. The classic picture is the dartboard: tight clustering away from the bullseye is reliable but not valid; tight clustering on the bullseye is both. Example: a bathroom scale that always reads 5 kg too heavy is reliable (consistent) but not valid (inaccurate).

Do not confuse test validity with research validity. Internal validity (Campbell and Stanley 1963) is whether the independent variable truly caused the change in the dependent variable; external validity is whether findings generalise to other people, settings and times. Generalizability theory (Cronbach et al. 1972) is an extension of reliability theory that splits error into multiple sources (raters, occasions, items).

Key distinctions

TermCore questionTypical index
ReliabilityIs it consistent?r tt, alpha, kappa
ValidityIs it accurate, does it measure the construct?Validity coefficient, factor analysis
Test-retest vs parallel formsStability over time vs equivalence of formsCoefficient of stability vs equivalence
Split-half vs alphaTwo halves (needs Spearman-Brown) vs all possible splitsSpearman-Brown, Cronbach's alpha
Concurrent vs predictiveCriterion now vs criterion laterBoth criterion-related
Convergent vs discriminantCorrelates with same construct vs not with differentBoth construct validity (MTMM)
Sensitivity vs specificityDetects true cases vs correctly rejects non-casesDiagnostic accuracy, not reliability

MCQ traps

  • "Consistency" is reliability, "accuracy/meaning" is validity. Validity is the most tempting distractor because both words sound like "good test".
  • Generalizability looks right because G-theory is a reliability framework, but the plain term for consistency is still reliability.
  • Sensitivity is a diagnostic-accuracy index (true positive rate), not consistency.
  • Face validity is not real psychometric validity; if asked which validity is "judged by experts on item coverage", it is content, not face.
  • "A reliable test is always valid" is false; "a valid test must be reliable" is true.
  • Spearman-Brown corrects split-half reliability; it is not a validity formula. KR-20 is for dichotomous (right/wrong) items, alpha for Likert items.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q75: Which term refers to the consistency of a measure? → Reliability, because reliability is by definition the consistency (stability, equivalence, internal agreement) of scores, while validity is about accuracy.
38Rorschach and projective tests
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q17

From scratch

A projective test presents an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus and asks the person to interpret it. The projective hypothesis holds that, because the stimulus has no single correct meaning, people impose their own needs, conflicts, defences and thought organisation onto it. Lawrence Frank (1939) coined the term "projective methods". The approach grew from psychoanalytic theory, and these tests are contrasted with objective (self-report) inventories like the MMPI, which have structured items, fixed response options and standardised scoring.

The Rorschach Inkblot Test. Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach published it in Psychodiagnostik (1921). It has 10 symmetrical inkblot cards: five achromatic (black and grey: I, IV, V, VI, VII), two black-and-red (II, III), and three fully chromatic (VIII, IX, X). Administration has two phases: the free association (response) phase, where the person says what each blot might be, and the inquiry phase, where the examiner asks where and what made it look that way. Responses are coded for location (W whole, D common detail, Dd unusual detail, S white space), determinants (form F, colour C, human movement M, shading, texture), content (human H, animal A, anatomy etc.), popular responses (P) and form quality (how well the percept fits the blot). Poor form quality and unusual verbalisations index disordered thinking, which is why the test is classically described as assessing thought processes and perceptual-cognitive organisation. The most used scoring system is John Exner's Comprehensive System (1974 onward), now succeeded by R-PAS (Rorschach Performance Assessment System, Meyer et al. 2011). Exner's Perceptual-Thinking Index is used to detect psychotic thought disorder. Earlier systems: Beck, Klopfer, Hertz, Piotrowski, Rapaport-Schafer.

Thematic Apperception Test (TAT). Henry Murray and Christiana Morgan (1935), Harvard, based on Murray's need-press theory. 31 cards (30 pictures plus one blank card, card 16); typically about 20 are given in two sessions. The person tells a story: what led up to the scene, what is happening, what the characters feel and think, how it ends. It assesses needs, motives, interpersonal themes rather than thought organisation. Children's versions: CAT (Bellak, animal figures), and the Indian adaptation by Uma Chowdhury (Indian TAT, CAT).

Other projectives, using Lindzey's (1959) classification by response type: association (Rorschach, Holtzman Inkblot Technique with 45 blots, two parallel forms, one response per card; Jung's word association test), construction (TAT, CAT), completion (Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank, Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study), choice or ordering (Szondi test), and expression (Draw-a-Person, Machover 1949; House-Tree-Person, Buck 1948; Kinetic Family Drawing).

Strengths and criticisms. Projectives are hard to fake, bypass defensiveness and give rich idiographic material. But they have been criticised for weak inter-scorer reliability, inconsistent norms and modest validity (Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb 2000). The Comprehensive System and R-PAS were built specifically to improve reliability and norms. Example: a patient who sees "a bleeding face melting into the walls" on card II with poor form quality suggests perceptual distortion; a TAT story full of failure and punishment suggests a theme of guilt.

Key distinctions

TestAuthor, yearStimulusMain target
RorschachHermann Rorschach, 192110 inkblotsPerception, thought processes, reality testing
HoltzmanHoltzman, 196145 inkblots, forms A and BSame, with better psychometrics
TATMurray and Morgan, 193531 pictures incl. blankNeeds, motives, themes (stories)
CATBellak, 1949Animal picturesChildren's conflicts
MMPIHathaway and McKinley, 1943True/false itemsObjective psychopathology profile
MBTIBriggs and MyersForced-choice itemsJungian type, objective, non-clinical

MCQ traps

  • TAT is the main distractor for "projective" questions; it uses pictures and stories, never inkblots.
  • MMPI assesses psychopathology but is objective, not projective.
  • MBTI is a self-report type indicator, not a clinical or projective tool.
  • Rorschach has 10 cards, TAT 31, Holtzman 45; "the blank card" belongs to TAT (card 16).
  • Rorschach is an association technique; TAT is construction; sentence completion is completion.
  • Exner developed the Comprehensive scoring system, not the test itself.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q17: Which personality assessment tool uses inkblots to assess thought processes? → Rorschach Test, the only inkblot test among the options; TAT uses pictures, MMPI and MBTI are objective inventories.
39Rater errors: halo, leniency, central tendency
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q54, Q15

From scratch

Whenever one person rates another (interviews, performance appraisals, rating scales, clinical impressions), the ratings are contaminated by systematic biases in the rater, not just random noise. These rater errors lower the reliability and validity of rating scales and are a standard topic in psychological assessment (Anastasi, Guilford) and in industrial-organisational psychology.

Halo effect (halo error). Named by Edward Thorndike (1920), who found that military officers' ratings of soldiers on separate traits (physique, intelligence, leadership, character) were far too highly correlated. Definition: the tendency to let one salient trait or a general overall impression colour the ratings of all other, unrelated traits. If a candidate is attractive and articulate, the interviewer also rates them as intelligent, honest and hard-working. The negative version is the horn (reverse halo) effect: one bad trait drags all ratings down. In social psychology the halo effect is a person-perception bias (Asch's central traits such as warm/cold work similarly; Dion, Berscheid and Walster 1972 showed "what is beautiful is good").

Leniency (generosity) error: rating everyone too favourably, crowding scores at the high end. Severity (strictness) error: rating everyone too harshly. Central tendency error: avoiding extremes and putting everyone in the middle ("average" on every item), often because the rater does not know the ratee well or wants to avoid justifying extreme ratings. All three restrict the range of ratings, so they reduce discrimination between people.

Other errors. Contrast error: rating someone relative to the person rated just before (an average candidate looks excellent after a weak one), or rating others as opposite to oneself. Similar-to-me error: rating higher those who resemble the rater in background or attitudes. Logical error (Guilford): rating traits similarly because the rater believes they logically go together (e.g., assuming the intelligent are also responsible). Proximity error: items close together on the form get similar ratings. Primacy (first-impression) and recency errors: early information or the most recent events dominate the judgment.

Remedies. Behaviourally anchored rating scales (BARS), forced-choice and forced-distribution methods (counter leniency and central tendency), rating all ratees on one trait at a time (counters halo), multiple independent raters, and rater training. Example: a ward supervisor who rates a nurse highly on punctuality, empathy and documentation simply because she is cheerful is committing halo error; one who gives every nurse 3 out of 5 is committing central tendency error.

Key distinctions

ErrorWhat happensCue word in stem
HaloOne trait or global impression spreads to all others"single attribute", "one positive trait"
HornOne negative trait spreads to all"one bad quality"
LeniencyEveryone rated high"too generous"
SeverityEveryone rated low"too harsh"
Central tendencyEveryone rated in the middle"avoids extremes"
ContrastJudged against the previous ratee or against oneself"compared with the previous candidate"
Similarity (similar-to-me)Favouring people like the rater"same background as rater"
Primacy / first impressionEarly information dominates later judgments"initial impressions"
Logical errorTraits assumed to co-occur are rated alike"believes traits go together"

MCQ traps

  • "Effect of initial impressions on subsequent judgments" describes the primacy effect, not halo. Halo is about one trait spilling over to other traits, not about order.
  • "Judging others based on group membership" is stereotyping, not halo.
  • Central tendency error is about clustering ratings in the middle, it has nothing to do with judging on one attribute, though the word "central" may tempt you.
  • Contrast error involves comparison with someone else; similarity error involves resemblance to the rater; neither is a single-trait spillover.
  • Do not confuse logical error (rater's belief that traits go together) with halo (an overall impression spreads), although both inflate inter-trait correlations.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q54: The tendency of a rater or interviewer to judge an individual on the basis of one single attribute → Halo error, because one salient attribute colours the whole evaluation; central tendency, contrast and similarity errors involve different mechanisms.
  • Q15: What is the halo effect? → The influence of a positive trait on the perception of other traits, Thorndike's definition; "initial impressions on subsequent judgments" is primacy and "group membership" is stereotyping.
40Indian tools (ISAA) and RPwD Act 2016 ★
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q85, Q96

From scratch

Indian entrance papers repeatedly test two linked areas: indigenous or Indian-adapted assessment tools, and the legal framework for disability certification. The law is the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016, which replaced the Persons with Disabilities Act, 1995 and brought Indian law in line with the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD, 2006). The 1995 Act recognised 7 disabilities; the 2016 Act recognises 21, adding among others autism spectrum disorder, specific learning disabilities (SLD), chronic neurological conditions, multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's disease, thalassaemia, haemophilia, sickle cell disease, acid attack victims, speech and language disability, and multiple disabilities. Intellectual disability, SLD and autism sit together under the "intellectual disability" group, while mental illness is a separate category.

Key provisions. A person with benchmark disability has at least 40% of a specified disability as certified by the certifying authority; this threshold unlocks benefits. Reservation is 4% in government jobs and at least 5% of seats in government and aided higher-education institutions; children with benchmark disabilities are entitled to free education from 6 to 18 years. Assessment and certification follow the Government of India guidelines notified in January 2018 (Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment), which name the tools and the boards for each disability.

SLD under the guidelines. SLD covers dyslexia, dysgraphia, dyscalculia, dyspraxia and developmental aphasia. A child cannot be reliably diagnosed until they have had enough formal schooling to show a persistent failure to acquire reading, writing or arithmetic despite average intelligence and adequate teaching. So the guidelines set the minimum age for SLD assessment and certification at 8 years. Below that, a child may be flagged as "at risk" and given remedial help, but not certified. Indian SLD tools include the NIMHANS Index for Specific Learning Disabilities (Kapur, Hirisave and colleagues), the Diagnostic Test of Learning Disability (Swarup and Mehta) and the Grade Level Assessment Device (GLAD).

Autism and the ISAA. The Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (ISAA) was developed by the National Institute for the Mentally Handicapped (NIMH, Secunderabad, now NIEPID) around 2009 and is the instrument prescribed for diagnosing autism and grading its severity and percentage of disability for certification. It has 40 items rated 1 to 5 across six domains: social relationship and reciprocity; emotional responsiveness; speech, language and communication; behaviour patterns; sensory aspects; and cognitive component. Total scores range 40 to 200: below 70 is no autism, 70 to 106 mild (40 to 60% disability), 107 to 153 moderate (61 to 80%), and above 153 severe (over 80%). It was built partly on CARS items, which is why CARS is the classic distractor. CARS (Childhood Autism Rating Scale, Schopler, Reichler and Renner, 1980) is American, 15 items, and rates severity but is not the Indian certification tool.

Other Indian tools worth knowing. VSMS (Vineland Social Maturity Scale, Indian adaptation by A. J. Malin) for social age and social quotient; MISIC (Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children, adaptation of WISC); Binet-Kamat Test (Kamat's adaptation of Stanford-Binet); Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests; Developmental Screening Test (Bharat Raj); NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery; PGI Memory Scale (Pershad and Wig); Seguin Form Board (used in Indian ID certification); IDEAS (Indian Disability Evaluation and Assessment Scale) for disability in mental illness.

Key distinctions

ItemCorrect factCommon confusion
ISAAIndian, NIMH 2009, 40 items, severity plus % disabilityCARS (American, 15 items)
CBCLAchenbach, broad emotional/behavioural checklistNot autism-specific
CGASChildren's Global Assessment Scale, general functioningNot autism-specific
IDEASDisability in mental illnessISAA is for autism
SLD minimum assessment age8 years7 (too early), 10, 14
Benchmark disability40%50%
Number of disabilities21 (2016 Act) vs 7 (1995 Act)Mixing the two Acts

MCQ traps

  • CARS vs ISAA: when the stem says "Indian tool", the textbook answer is ISAA; CARS is American even though ISAA borrowed from it.
  • 7 years feels right because schooling starts around 6, but the guideline minimum for SLD certification is 8.
  • 21 vs 7: 21 disabilities is RPwD 2016; 7 is the 1995 PwD Act.
  • The 40% benchmark, 4% jobs reservation, 5% higher-education seats: distractors swap these numbers.
  • IQ below 70 is not an autism criterion (DSM-5 codes intellectual disability separately), so do not assume autism certification requires low IQ.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q85: Name of the Indian tool to assess the severity and disability of children with autism → official key Childhood Autism Rating Scale; this key is debated. The textbook-correct answer is ISAA, the NIMH-developed Indian scale prescribed for autism severity and disability certification. In the exam, go with the key if you must guess the setter's intent, but know ISAA is the Indian tool.
  • Q96: Minimum age for the assessment of children with specific learning disability as per RPwD Act 2016 → 8 years, because SLD can only be separated from normal developmental lag after enough formal schooling.
  • STAR: Q96 is the one you missed. You picked one of the other ages (7, 10 or 14); lock in 8 years for SLD certification.
41Graphs and frequency distributions ★
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q21, Q53, Q38, Q37

From scratch

Descriptive statistics begins by organising raw data. Raw scores are the original, unprocessed measurements. Listing each value with how often it occurs gives a frequency distribution. When there are many distinct values, scores are collapsed into class intervals (e.g., 40 to 44, 45 to 49) to form a grouped frequency distribution. Each interval has stated limits (40 and 44), exact or real limits (39.5 and 44.5) and a midpoint (42). Common rules (King and Minium, Garrett): use roughly 10 to 20 intervals of equal width, and choose convenient widths such as 2, 3, 5 or 10. Because the researcher chooses the width and starting point, a set of raw scores does not result in a unique set of grouped scores: the same data can be grouped many ways, and grouping loses information (you no longer know exact scores within an interval).

Kinds of frequency. Frequency (f) is the count in a category. Cumulative frequency (cf) is the running total up to the upper limit of each interval, used for percentiles and medians. Relative frequency is f divided by N, expressed as a proportion (or multiplied by 100 as a percentage). Relative frequencies are especially useful when comparing distributions with different numbers of cases: if Class A has 200 students and Class B has 50, raw counts are not comparable, but proportions put both on the same base. Cumulative relative frequency (cumulative percentage) combines both ideas.

Graphs. (1) Histogram: a series of adjacent rectangles, one per class interval, with height equal to frequency; bars touch because the variable is continuous (interval or ratio data). (2) Bar graph: separated bars for discrete or categorical (nominal, ordinal) data such as diagnosis type. (3) Frequency polygon: points plotted at interval midpoints joined by straight lines, closed at both ends; best for comparing two distributions on one graph. (4) Ogive: the cumulative frequency (or cumulative percentage) curve, S-shaped, plotted at upper exact limits; used to read percentiles and the median. (5) Pie chart: sectors showing parts of a whole for categorical data. (6) Stem-and-leaf display (Tukey 1977): keeps raw values while showing shape.

Scatterplot (scatter diagram). Used for bivariate correlational data: each person is one point, X score on the horizontal axis and Y score on the vertical. An upward-sloping cloud means positive correlation, downward means negative, a shapeless cloud means near zero; the tighter the cloud around a line, the stronger the relationship. Francis Galton used such plots in the 1880s to study regression, and Karl Pearson formalised the correlation coefficient r. Scatterplots also reveal curvilinearity (e.g., the inverted-U of the Yerkes-Dodson law) and outliers that r alone would hide.

Shapes. Normal (bell curve, symmetric, mean = median = mode), positively skewed (tail to the right, mean above median), negatively skewed (tail to the left), and kurtosis: leptokurtic (peaked), mesokurtic (normal), platykurtic (flat).

Key distinctions

Graph or termUseDo not confuse with
HistogramContinuous grouped data, touching rectanglesBar graph (gaps, categorical)
Frequency polygonMidpoints joined by lines; comparing distributionsOgive (cumulative)
OgiveCumulative frequency; percentiles, medianPolygon
ScatterplotTwo variables per person; correlationBell curve (one variable's shape)
Relative frequencyf/N; compares unequal-sized groupsCumulative frequency (running total, still raw counts)
Raw scoresUngrouped originals; can be grouped in many waysGrouped scores

MCQ traps

  • Cumulative frequencies sound like a comparison tool, but they stay on the raw count scale, so they do not solve unequal N. The answer for unequal N is relative frequencies.
  • "Frequency percentage" and "frequency proportion" are informal variants of relative frequency; when the textbook term is offered, pick relative frequencies.
  • Bar graph vs histogram: "rectangles for class intervals" means histogram; separated bars for categories means bar graph.
  • Chi-square appears as a distractor for "graph of correlational data"; it is a test statistic, not a graph. A bell curve shows the shape of one variable.
  • "Frequency polygon" is plotted at midpoints; the ogive at upper exact limits.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q21: A graphical representation of correlational data is called a → Scatterplot, since each point pairs one person's two scores.
  • Q53: Data shown as a series of rectangles, each representing scores in one class interval → Histogram, adjacent bars on a continuous scale.
  • Q38: ___ are particularly helpful when comparing frequency distributions in which the number of cases differs → Relative frequencies, because dividing by N puts both distributions on a common base.
  • Q37: A set of ___ scores does not result in a unique set of grouped scores → official key Raw; this item is listed as debated, but the key matches the textbook (King and Minium): raw data can be grouped many ways depending on interval width and starting point.
  • STAR: Q38 is the one you missed. You picked one of the near-synonyms or cumulative frequencies; the standard term is relative frequencies.
42Research designs
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q24, Q29, Q60, Q81, Q86

From scratch

A research design is the plan that links a question to the data. The central divide is between experimental designs, which can establish cause and effect, and non-experimental designs (correlational, survey, observational, case study), which describe or predict but cannot prove causation. In a true experiment the researcher (1) manipulates an independent variable (IV), (2) measures its effect on a dependent variable (DV), and (3) controls other influences, chiefly through random assignment. Example: 60 patients with depression are randomly assigned to CBT or a waitlist; if the CBT group improves more on the BDI, the improvement can be attributed to CBT.

Randomisation vs random sampling. Random sampling (random selection) is how people are drawn from the population into the study, so every member has an equal chance of being included; it protects external validity (generalisation). Randomisation (random assignment) is how participants already in the study are allocated to treatment groups so each has an equal probability of any group; it equates groups on all pre-existing variables, known and unknown, and protects internal validity. R. A. Fisher (1935, The Design of Experiments) made randomisation the foundation of experimental design. Stratification divides the population into subgroups (strata) first, then samples within each.

Confounding variables. A confound is an extraneous variable that is related to both the IV and the DV, so its effect cannot be separated from the IV's. If the CBT group happened to be younger and the young recover faster, age confounds the result. Controls: random assignment, holding variables constant, matching, counterbalancing (for order effects in within-subjects designs), blinding (single-blind, double-blind against expectancy and demand characteristics), and statistical control (ANCOVA). A quasi-experiment (Campbell and Stanley 1963; Cook and Campbell 1979) has manipulation or comparison groups but no random assignment, so confounds remain plausible.

Developmental designs. To study change with age: a cross-sectional design tests different age groups at one time (20-, 40-, 60-year-olds in 2026). It is quick and cheap but confounds age with cohort: the groups differ in generation, schooling, nutrition and historical experience, so differences may be cohort differences, not ageing (this is why early cross-sectional studies exaggerated IQ decline). A longitudinal design follows the same people over time, directly showing intra-individual change and stability, but it is slow and costly, and suffers attrition (dropout), practice effects and time-of-measurement effects. K. Warner Schaie's Seattle Longitudinal Study used a cross-sequential (sequential) design, combining both, to separate age, cohort and time effects.

Other designs. Correlational: measures how variables co-vary (r), no manipulation, cannot rule out third variables or reverse causation. Case study: intensive study of one person (Freud's Anna O, H.M.), rich but low generalisability. Between-subjects (different people per condition) vs within-subjects / repeated measures (same people in all conditions, needs counterbalancing). Single-case experimental designs (ABA, ABAB, multiple baseline) are used in behaviour therapy.

Key distinctions

PairFirstSecond
Random sampling vs randomisationWho gets into the study; external validityWho gets which treatment; internal validity
Experimental vs correlationalManipulation plus random assignment; causationNo manipulation; association only
Confound vs extraneous variableVaries systematically with IV and affects DVAny outside variable, may just add noise
Cross-sectional vs longitudinalDifferent people, one time; cohort effectSame people, many times; attrition, practice
True vs quasi-experimentRandom assignmentNo random assignment

MCQ traps

  • Random sampling is the trap for any stem about assigning people to treatment groups; that is randomisation.
  • "A variable that only affects the DV" is just an extraneous influence; a confound must be linked to both IV and DV.
  • For the disadvantage of cross-sectional studies, attrition is the lure, but attrition is a longitudinal problem; the cross-sectional problem is cohort difference.
  • "Changes over time" means longitudinal, even though a cross-sectional study also compares ages.
  • Correlational studies can show strong relationships but not cause; only the experiment gets "cause and effect".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q24: ___ ensures assignment of subjects to treatment groups so every member has equal probability of any assignment → Randomization, which allocates to groups, unlike random sampling which selects into the study.
  • Q29: What is a confounding variable? → A variable that influences both the independent and dependent variables, so its effect is tangled with the IV's.
  • Q60: Design best suited for cause and effect → Experimental design, because only manipulation plus control and random assignment rule out alternatives.
  • Q81: A disadvantage of cross-sectional studies is that results can be influenced by → Cohort difference, since the age groups also differ by generation.
  • Q86: Design best suited for examining changes over time → Longitudinal design, which follows the same people repeatedly.
43Chi-square df and p-value
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q84, Q90

From scratch

The chi-square test (χ²), introduced by Karl Pearson (1900), is a non-parametric test for frequency (count) data in nominal or ordinal categories. It compares observed frequencies (O) with the expected frequencies (E) predicted by the null hypothesis: χ² = Σ (O minus E)² / E. If observed counts are close to expected, χ² is small; large discrepancies give a large χ² and a small p-value.

Two main uses. (1) Goodness of fit (one variable): do observed counts fit a hypothesised distribution? Example: are patients equally spread across four OPD days? Here df = k minus 1, where k is the number of categories (so 4 days gives df = 3). (2) Test of independence (two variables in a contingency table): is gender related to diagnosis? Expected frequency for each cell = (row total × column total) / N, and df = (number of rows minus 1) × (number of columns minus 1). A 2 × 2 table has df = 1; a 3 × 4 table has df = 2 × 3 = 6. The logic: once the row and column totals are fixed, only (r minus 1)(c minus 1) cells are free to vary. Assumptions: independent observations (each person counted once), and expected frequency of at least 5 in each cell (in practice no more than 20% of cells below 5). For a 2 × 2 table with small expected counts, use Yates' correction for continuity or Fisher's exact test. The chi-square distribution is positively skewed and its mean equals its df. Effect size for association: phi (2 × 2) or Cramér's V.

The p-value. In null hypothesis significance testing (Fisher; Neyman and Pearson), you assume H0 (no effect, no association). The p-value is the probability of obtaining data as extreme as, or more extreme than, those observed, assuming the null hypothesis is true: P(data | H0). If p is less than the pre-set alpha (usually 0.05), the result is "statistically significant" and H0 is rejected. Example: χ²(1) = 6.2, p = .013 means that if gender and diagnosis were truly unrelated, a discrepancy this large would arise only about 1.3% of the time by sampling error.

What p is not. It is not the probability that H0 is true, not the probability that the result was "due to chance", not 1 minus the probability the alternative hypothesis is true, and not the size or importance of the effect. A tiny effect in a huge sample can have p below .001. That is why the APA (and the ASA statement, 2016) asks for effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside p.

Related terms. Alpha = probability of a Type I error (rejecting a true H0, false positive). Beta = probability of a Type II error (failing to reject a false H0, false negative). Power = 1 minus beta, the probability of detecting a real effect; it rises with larger N, larger effect size and higher alpha (Cohen recommended 0.80).

Key distinctions

ConceptFormula or meaning
df, chi-square independence(rows minus 1)(columns minus 1)
df, chi-square goodness of fitk minus 1
df, one-sample or paired tN minus 1
df, independent tN1 + N2 minus 2
df, Pearson rN minus 2
p-valueP(data this extreme | H0 true)
AlphaCut-off chosen in advance; Type I error rate
Power1 minus beta
Effect sizeMagnitude (d, r, phi, Cramér's V), independent of N

MCQ traps

  • (N minus 1) is the df for goodness-of-fit with N categories or for a one-sample t; it is not the contingency-table formula.
  • (N minus 2) is the df for correlation or independent-samples t, a different test.
  • (columns minus 2)(rows minus 2) is a made-up formula; always subtract 1.
  • "The probability that the null hypothesis is true" is the most popular wrong p-value definition; p is about the data given H0, not H0 given the data.
  • p is not the effect size and not power; a significant result can be trivial in size.
  • Chi-square is non-parametric and uses frequencies, never means.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q84: Correct formula for degrees of freedom in a chi-square test for independence → (N columns minus 1)(N rows minus 1), because with margins fixed only that many cells are free to vary.
  • Q90: What is the p-value? → The probability of obtaining the observed data if the null hypothesis is true; it is a conditional probability of the data, not of the hypothesis.
44Meta-analysis
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q87

From scratch

Meta-analysis is a quantitative method for statistically combining the results of many independent studies on the same question to produce an overall estimate of an effect. The term was coined by Gene V. Glass (1976), who called it "the analysis of analyses". Its landmark use in clinical psychology was Smith and Glass (1977), which pooled about 375 psychotherapy outcome studies and found that the average treated client was better off than about 75% of untreated controls (mean effect size around 0.68); Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) extended this to 475 studies (effect size about 0.85). This was a direct empirical reply to Hans Eysenck's (1952) claim that psychotherapy was no better than spontaneous remission; Eysenck in turn dismissed meta-analysis as "mega-silliness" and "garbage in, garbage out".

How it works, step by step. (1) Define a precise question (e.g., does CBT reduce depression compared with waitlist?). (2) Conduct a systematic literature search with explicit inclusion and exclusion criteria (reported using PRISMA guidelines). (3) Extract a common effect size from each study: Cohen's d or Hedges' g for mean differences (0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large), r for correlations, odds ratio or risk ratio for binary outcomes. (4) Pool them as a weighted average, giving more weight to larger, more precise studies (inverse-variance weighting). (5) Assess heterogeneity (Cochran's Q, the I² statistic) and choose a fixed-effect model (one true effect, differences are sampling error) or a random-effects model (true effects vary across studies). (6) Run moderator analyses (does the effect differ by age, severity, therapist training?). (7) Check publication bias.

Displays and biases. A forest plot shows each study's effect and confidence interval plus the pooled diamond. A funnel plot plots effect size against study precision; asymmetry suggests publication bias, because small non-significant studies tend to stay unpublished. Robert Rosenthal (1979) named this the file-drawer problem and proposed the fail-safe N (how many null studies would be needed to cancel the effect).

Systematic review vs meta-analysis. A systematic review is the structured, reproducible search and appraisal of all relevant studies; a meta-analysis is the statistical pooling step that may (or may not) follow. A narrative review summarises studies in words, without systematic search or statistics. Meta-analyses of randomised controlled trials sit at the top of the evidence hierarchy (Cochrane Collaboration), which is why they guide evidence-based practice and clinical guidelines (e.g., NICE). Criticisms: "apples and oranges" (mixing studies that measure different things), garbage in garbage out (poor-quality studies), and publication bias.

Key point: meta-analysis uses secondary data; it never collects new primary data and is not one large new experiment. Example: a 2026 meta-analysis of 40 RCTs on mindfulness for anxiety reporting pooled g = 0.45, I² = 60% (moderate heterogeneity) and a funnel plot hinting at missing small negative studies.

Key distinctions

MethodWhat it doesData
Meta-analysisStatistically pools effect sizes across studiesSecondary, quantitative
Systematic reviewSystematic search and appraisal, may be narrativeSecondary
Narrative reviewQualitative summary, no systematic searchSecondary
Multi-centre RCT / mega-trialOne large new experiment across sitesPrimary
Case studyIn-depth study of one individualPrimary, idiographic
Fixed vs random effectsOne true effect vs a distribution of true effectsModel choice
Forest vs funnel plotPooled effect display vs publication-bias checkGraphs

MCQ traps

  • "To conduct a single large experiment" describes a mega-trial or multi-centre RCT, not meta-analysis, which combines already-completed studies.
  • "To collect primary data" is what original studies do; meta-analysis uses secondary data.
  • The funnel plot detects publication bias; the forest plot shows pooled results. Exams swap them.
  • Glass coined the term (1976); Eysenck was the critic, not the founder; Rosenthal named the file-drawer problem.
  • A systematic review is not automatically a meta-analysis; only the statistical pooling makes it one.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q87: Main purpose of meta-analysis in clinical psychology → official key To conduct a single large experiment; this key is debated and is wrong by textbook standards. The textbook-correct answer is To summarize findings from multiple studies, because meta-analysis statistically pools effect sizes from existing studies. Learn the concept; note the paper's key.
45Schools: Wundt + Titchener (structuralism), James (functionalism), Watson (behaviourism), G. Stanley Hall
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q11, Q73, Q92, Q7 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 1 gave the headline sequence. Here are the people, the books and the details that MCQs pull out as options.

Wilhelm Wundt (1832 to 1920) wrote Principles of Physiological Psychology (1873 to 1874), the first textbook of psychology as a laboratory science, and opened the Leipzig laboratory in 1879. He also founded the first psychology journal, Philosophische Studien (1881). His own name for his system was voluntarism, because he stressed the will and apperception (actively focusing attention so that elements are combined by creative synthesis into something new). His tridimensional theory of feeling described every feeling on three axes: pleasant to unpleasant, tension to relaxation, excitement to calm. Wundt believed the laboratory could study only simple processes; higher mental processes (language, myth, custom) had to be studied historically and culturally, which he did in the ten-volume Volkerpsychologie (folk or cultural psychology, 1900 to 1920). So Wundt was broader than the "structuralist" label textbooks give him.

Edward Bradford Titchener (1867 to 1927), an Englishman trained by Wundt, worked at Cornell. He named his system structuralism in his 1898 paper "The postulates of a structural psychology", to contrast it with functionalism. He narrowed Wundt: three elements (sensations, images, affections), with affection having only one dimension (pleasant to unpleasant). His Experimental Psychology manuals (1901 to 1905) trained a generation of American laboratory workers. Structuralism ended with his death in 1927.

William James (1842 to 1910), at Harvard, wrote The Principles of Psychology (1890) and the shorter Psychology: Briefer Course (1892, nicknamed "Jimmy" by students, versus "the James" for the big book). Key ideas: stream of consciousness, habit as the "flywheel of society", the self (material, social, spiritual and pure ego), the James-Lange theory of emotion (we feel afraid because we tremble), and pragmatism (an idea is true if it works). James is the inspiration of functionalism, though he never founded a formal school; the organised school was at Chicago (Dewey, Angell, Carr) and Columbia (Cattell, Woodworth, Thorndike).

John B. Watson (1878 to 1958), trained at Chicago, launched behaviourism in his 1913 paper "Psychology as the behaviorist views it" (the "behaviorist manifesto"), at Johns Hopkins. Goals: prediction and control of behaviour; method: observation of stimulus and response; no introspection. He treated thinking as subvocal speech and emotions as conditioned responses (Little Albert, with Rosalie Rayner, 1920). His famous "give me a dozen healthy infants" claim appeared in Behaviorism (1924/1925). After leaving Johns Hopkins in 1920 he worked in advertising. Watson's version is methodological behaviourism; Skinner's radical behaviourism later counted private events (thoughts) as behaviour too.

G. Stanley Hall (1844 to 1924) is the "firsts" man of American psychology: the first American PhD in psychology (Harvard, 1878, under James), studied with Wundt, opened one of the first American psychology labs at Johns Hopkins (1883), founded the American Journal of Psychology (1887), became the first president of the APA (1892), and as president of Clark University invited Freud and Jung to lecture in 1909. His book Adolescence (1904) described the teenage years as "storm and stress", and he applied recapitulation theory (each child relives the stages of human evolution). He founded the child-study movement. He belongs loosely with the functionalist, developmental tradition, not to any one school.

Key distinctions

PersonSchool / labelSignature fact
WundtVoluntarism (textbooks: structuralism's father)Leipzig 1879, apperception, Volkerpsychologie
TitchenerStructuralism (named it, 1898)Cornell, 3 elements, stimulus error
JamesFunctionalism (inspirer)Principles 1890, stream of consciousness, James-Lange
Dewey, Angell, CarrFunctionalism (Chicago school)Reflex arc paper (Dewey, 1896)
WatsonMethodological behaviourism1913 manifesto, Little Albert
SkinnerRadical behaviourismOperant conditioning, Skinner box
G. Stanley HallNo single school; developmental pioneerFirst US PhD, APA president 1892, Adolescence 1904

MCQ traps

  • Titchener, not Wundt, coined "structuralism". If the stem asks who named or systematised it, pick Titchener.
  • Hall is a "firsts" distractor (APA, journal, PhD). He is not the founder of functionalism or behaviourism.
  • Watson vs Skinner: Watson = founder, methodological, 1913; Skinner = radical behaviourism, operant. Q7 tests the Skinner label.
  • "Voluntarism" means Wundt, not William James, even though James wrote much about the will.
  • Stream of consciousness is James. Wundt and Titchener are the opposite view (elements), so they are the usual wrong options.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q11: Functionalism was most closely associated with → William James; Titchener (structuralism), Watson (behaviourism) and G. Stanley Hall (first APA president, adolescence) were the three distractors from this topic.
  • Q73: Who coined "stream of consciousness" → William James; Wundt sat as a distractor because his introspective, element-based approach is the view James was arguing against.
  • Q92: Theory emphasising internal mental processes → Cognitive psychology; behaviourism (Watson) was the distractor because it deliberately excludes mental processes.
  • Q7: Match theorists to theories → Skinner = radical behaviourism (Dollard and Miller = early social learning, Bandura and Walters = later social learning, Maslow = self-actualisation).
46Nature vs nurture: Plato, Descartes, Kant vs Locke
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q27 · added from the options

From scratch

The nature-nurture question asks how much of the mind is given at birth (nature, heredity, innate structure) and how much is built by experience (nurture, environment, learning). Psychology inherited it from philosophy, where it was the battle between nativism/rationalism and empiricism.

Plato (about 427 to 347 BCE) was the first great nativist. In the dialogue Meno, Socrates questions an uneducated slave boy, who, guided only by questions, works out a geometry proof. Plato concluded that knowledge is innate: the soul knew the eternal Forms before birth, and learning is really recollection (anamnesis). The senses give only imperfect copies of the Forms. His student Aristotle moved toward empiricism, comparing the mind to a wax tablet on which experience writes, and gave the first laws of association (contiguity, similarity, contrast).

Rene Descartes (1596 to 1650), the father of modern rationalism, argued in the Meditations (1641) that some ideas (God, the self, infinity, the axioms of geometry) cannot come from the senses and must be innate ideas. He is also the source of mind-body dualism (an immaterial thinking mind interacting with a mechanical body, he suggested through the pineal gland) and of the mechanical reflex idea that later fed physiology.

John Locke (1632 to 1704), in An Essay Concerning Human Understanding (1690), attacked innate ideas directly. The mind at birth is "white paper", the tabula rasa. Ideas come from sensation and reflection; simple ideas combine into complex ones. He separated primary qualities (in the object: size, shape, motion) from secondary qualities (in the perceiver: colour, taste). Locke started British empiricism (Berkeley, Hume, Hartley, the Mills), which became associationism and then learning theory and behaviourism. Leibniz replied that the mind is more like veined marble: experience carves it, but the grain is already there.

Immanuel Kant (1724 to 1804), in the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), offered a synthesis. Knowledge begins with experience, but the mind brings a priori forms (space and time) and categories (causality, unity, substance) that organise raw sensation. In his phrase, "thoughts without content are empty, intuitions without concepts are blind." Kant is counted on the nature side in MCQs because the organising structure is innate. His view inspired Gestalt psychology and, later, Piaget's idea of active mental structures.

In psychology, Francis Galton made the phrase "nature and nurture" popular (English Men of Science, 1874), argued for heredity in Hereditary Genius (1869) and proposed the twin method. Watson's "dozen healthy infants" boast is the extreme nurture claim; Gesell's maturation theory leaned to nature. The modern answer is interaction: heritability estimates from twin and adoption studies, gene-environment correlation and interaction, the diathesis-stress model, and epigenetics (experience changes gene expression). A heritability of .50 describes variation in a population, not "half of one person's trait".

Key distinctions

ThinkerSideKey phrase
PlatoNature (nativist)Innate knowledge, learning is recollection
AristotleLeans nurtureWax tablet, laws of association
DescartesNature (rationalist)Innate ideas, dualism
LockeNurture (empiricist)Tabula rasa, sensation and reflection
LeibnizNatureVeined marble
KantNature (synthesis)A priori categories organise experience
GaltonNature (heredity)Coined "nature and nurture", twin method

MCQ traps

  • Tabula rasa = Locke, even though the image traces back to Aristotle's wax tablet. In options, always Locke.
  • Kant is not an empiricist just because he said knowledge begins with experience; the categories are innate, so he is the nativist/synthesis option.
  • Descartes appears for both dualism and innate ideas; neither makes him a nurture theorist.
  • Rousseau's "noble savage" is about natural goodness, not the blank slate; do not confuse with Locke.
  • Heritability is a population statistic, not a fixed share of an individual's trait.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q27: Which early philosopher held the mind is a "tabula rasa" at birth → John Locke. All three distractors came from this topic and all three are nativists: Plato (innate Forms, recollection), Descartes (innate ideas) and Kant (a priori categories). The question is solved by knowing which camp each belongs to.
47Sheldon's 3 somatotypes in full (endo / meso / ecto)
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q8 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 9 introduced Sheldon briefly. This is the full version, because MCQs quote his temperament adjectives word for word.

William H. Sheldon, an American physician and psychologist, built a constitutional theory of personality: body build and temperament are both expressions of an underlying biological constitution. In The Varieties of Human Physique (1940) he photographed about 4,000 male college students from the front, side and back under standard conditions. In The Varieties of Temperament (1942) he rated temperament, and in Varieties of Delinquent Youth (1949) and the Atlas of Men (1954) he extended the work.

The three components and why they have those names. Sheldon named each component after the embryonic germ layer whose tissues dominate that body:

  • Endomorphy (endoderm → digestive tract and viscera): soft, round, a large abdomen, weak bones and muscles, a "pear" shape.
  • Mesomorphy (mesoderm → bone, muscle, heart, blood vessels): hard, rectangular, heavy muscle and bone, broad shoulders, narrow waist.
  • Ectomorphy (ectoderm → skin, nervous system, sense organs): thin, long-limbed, fragile, flat-chested, the largest skin surface and brain relative to body mass.

Somatotyping. These are dimensions, not boxes. Each person is rated 1 to 7 on each component, giving a three-number somatotype in the order endo-meso-ecto: 7-1-1 extreme endomorph, 1-7-1 extreme mesomorph, 1-1-7 extreme ectomorph, 4-4-4 average. Sheldon also used secondary components such as dysplasia (different regions of the body having different somatotypes) and gynandromorphy (presence of features typical of the other sex). The modern anthropometric version is the Heath-Carter method (1967), still used in sports science.

The three temperaments. Sheldon began with 650 trait words, reduced them to 60 traits, 20 per component, and rated men on them in long interviews:

PhysiqueTemperamentSignature traits (Sheldon's terms)When troubled, needs...
EndomorphViscerotoniaRelaxed posture, love of physical comfort, love of eating, sociophilia, amiability, tolerance, complacency, slow reaction, deep sleepPeople
MesomorphSomatotoniaAssertive posture, love of physical adventure and exercise, energetic, love of dominance and risk, bold directness, competitive aggressiveness, psychological callousness, unrestrained (loud) voice, claustrophobiaAction
EctomorphCerebrotoniaRestraint, fast reactions, love of privacy, mental over-intensity, apprehensiveness, secretive feelings, self-consciousness, sociophobia, vocal restraint, sensitivity to pain, poor sleep, chronic fatigueSolitude

Sheldon reported correlations of about .80 between physique and temperament. The weakness: he rated both himself, so his knowledge of a man's body could bias his temperament ratings. Independent studies (for example Child, 1950) found correlations in the predicted direction but much weaker. In Varieties of Delinquent Youth he reported that delinquent boys were more mesomorphic, a finding partly echoed by the Gluecks (1950), but with social explanations equally likely (muscular boys get rewarded for toughness). He also linked extreme components to psychiatric patterns, a speculative claim not supported today.

Lineage. Sheldon refined Kretschmer's types (1921): pyknic ≈ endomorph, athletic ≈ mesomorph, asthenic/leptosomic ≈ ectomorph. Kretschmer linked them to psychosis (pyknic → manic-depressive, asthenic → schizophrenia); Sheldon linked them to normal temperament.

Key distinctions

  • Viscerotonia vs somatotonia: both may be "sociable", but viscerotonics seek comfort and affection, somatotonics seek dominance and action.
  • Cerebrotonia is not "intelligent"; it is restrained, inhibited, hypersensitive. The name refers to the nervous system, not IQ.
  • Sheldon vs Kretschmer: Sheldon = continuous 1 to 7 ratings of normal men; Kretschmer = categories linked to psychiatric illness.
  • Type vs trait: Sheldon is taught under type theories, but his ratings are actually dimensional.

MCQ traps

  • "Noisy, callous, fond of physical activity, assertive, adventurous" → mesomorph (somatotonia).
  • "Loves food, comfort, people, relaxed, even-tempered" → endomorph (viscerotonia).
  • "Restrained, private, sensitive, prefers solitude, fatigues easily" → ectomorph (cerebrotonia).
  • Do not pair mesomorph with cerebrotonia because "meso" sounds central; the germ-layer link is the reliable route.
  • The order in a somatotype code is always endo-meso-ecto.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q8: Which type is described as noisy, callous and fond of physical activity → Mesomorph (somatotonia: unrestrained voice, psychological callousness, love of physical adventure). Endomorph was the distractor because viscerotonics are relaxed comfort-lovers, and ectomorph because cerebrotonics are restrained and quiet. "None of the above" fails because the description is a textbook somatotonic.
48Culture and perception: Bruner & Goodman coin study, Segall + Herskovits (Müller-Lyer, carpentered world)
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q10, Q9 · added from the options

From scratch

Two research lines show that perception is not identical across people. The New Look showed that needs and values shape perception within one culture; the cross-cultural illusion studies showed that the visual environment a person grows up in shapes it across cultures.

Bruner and Goodman (1947), "Value and need as organizing factors in perception". Ten-year-old children in Boston adjusted the diameter of a circle of light to match coins (1, 5, 10, 25 and 50 cents) held in the palm. A control group matched grey cardboard discs of the same sizes. Coins were overestimated, discs were not; the more valuable the coin, the bigger the overestimation (except the 50-cent piece); and poor children overestimated more than rich children. Conclusion: economic need and social value enlarge perceived size. Follow-ups: Carter and Schooler (1949) found the effect mainly when judging from memory, not with the coin present; Ashley, Harper and Runyon (1951) hypnotised adults to feel "poor" or "rich" and found the "poor" state increased coin-size estimates. The study launched the New Look, which treats perception as top-down and motivated.

Segall, Campbell and Herskovits (paper 1963; book The Influence of Culture on Visual Perception, 1966) tested nearly 2,000 people across many societies (mostly African groups, plus the Philippines and Evanston, USA) on geometric illusions. Marshall Segall was the psychologist, Donald Campbell the methodologist, and Melville Herskovits the anthropologist known for cultural relativism. Their key finding: Western urban samples were more susceptible to the Müller-Lyer illusion (the line with outward fins looks longer) than many rural African samples.

The carpentered-world hypothesis explains this. People raised among rectangular buildings, straight edges and right-angled corners learn to read acute and obtuse angles in a 2D image as right angles receding in depth. The outward-fin figure resembles the inside corner of a room (far away), the inward-fin figure an outside corner of a building (near). Applying size constancy (Gregory's "misapplied size constancy" account), the "far" line is judged longer. People living in round huts with curved fields, like the Zulu, have less of this habit and show a smaller illusion. Related: Allport and Pettigrew (1957) found rural Zulu less likely to see the rotating trapezoidal window as a rectangle swinging back and forth.

The same study tested the horizontal-vertical illusion (the vertical looks longer) and proposed the foreshortening hypothesis: people from open plains, where vertical lines on the retina stand for long receding distances, are more susceptible than people living in dense forest. Other classics: Turnbull (1961), whose BaMbuti guide Kenge, raised in the forest, saw distant buffalo as insects (poor size constancy at long distance); Hudson (1960), showing that some unschooled African viewers did not read pictorial depth cues in drawings.

Key distinctions

StudyVariableFinding
Bruner and Goodman, 1947Need and value (poverty)Poor children overestimate coin size more
Segall, Campbell, Herskovits, 1963/1966Ecology (carpentered world)Western samples more susceptible to Müller-Lyer
Allport and Pettigrew, 1957Rectangular environmentRural Zulu less prone to trapezoidal window illusion
Turnbull, 1961Forest life, no long vistasSize constancy fails at distance
Hudson, 1960Schooling, picturesPictorial depth not read by some unschooled viewers

MCQ traps

  • "Economic deprivation affects perception" → Bruner and Goodman, not Segall or Herskovits, who studied culture and ecology, not poverty.
  • Herskovits was an anthropologist, co-author with Segall and Campbell; options often list "Segall" and "Herskovits" separately to tempt you.
  • Carpentered world predicts more illusion for city dwellers, not less.
  • Müller-Lyer vs horizontal-vertical: carpentered world explains the first, foreshortening (open vistas) the second.
  • The New Look is top-down processing; Gibson's direct perception is the bottom-up opposite.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q10: Who demonstrated that economic deprivation affects perception → Bruner and Goodman (coin study). Segall and "Hers Kovits" (Herskovits) were distractors from the cross-cultural illusion work, which concerns ecology and the carpentered world, not poverty. Broota and Ganguli, an Indian pairing, was the other distractor; it is not the classic deprivation study.
  • Q9: Perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events → Titchener; Bruner appeared only as a distractor here, since his New Look stresses needs and values, not sensory plus imaginal elements.
49All the Gestalt laws: continuity, similarity, closure
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q52 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 4 listed the laws. This topic drills the three that are most often confused with each other and with proximity: continuity, similarity and closure, plus the rest of the full list, so any one of them can be recognised from a one-line description.

Where the laws come from. Max Wertheimer set out the principles of grouping in his 1923 paper "Laws of organization in perceptual forms". Koffka (Principles of Gestalt Psychology, 1935) and Kohler (Gestalt Psychology, 1929) spread them. All are expressions of one master principle, Pragnanz (the law of good form or simplicity): the perceptual field organises itself into the simplest, most regular, most stable whole the stimulus allows. The laws describe grouping: which parts of a scene the brain treats as belonging together, before any recognition of what the objects are.

Law of continuity (good continuation). Elements arranged along a smooth line or curve are grouped, and we prefer interpretations in which lines keep their direction. Two lines crossing to form an X are seen as two straight lines, not as two V shapes touching at the tip. A wire running behind a pole is seen as one wire. In camouflage, a continuous contour running across an animal's outline breaks the figure apart. The key word is direction or flow.

Law of similarity. Elements that look alike (in colour, shape, size, orientation, texture or brightness) are grouped. A grid of evenly spaced dots in which alternate rows are filled and empty is seen as rows, even though spacing is identical. Team jerseys in a football match let you see two groups. The key word is alike. When similarity and proximity conflict, the result depends on which difference is stronger.

Law of closure. We tend to complete incomplete figures, filling gaps to see a whole object. A circle drawn with a few breaks is seen as a circle; the WWF panda is seen although large parts of its outline are missing. The Kanizsa triangle (Gaetano Kanizsa, 1955) goes further: three "Pac-Man" discs produce a bright white triangle with illusory (subjective) contours that do not exist on the page. The key word is incomplete or gaps.

The other laws. Proximity: items close together in space or time form a group (the key word is near). Common fate: items moving in the same direction at the same speed group (a flock of birds). Symmetry: symmetrical regions are seen as figures and grouped. Past experience (familiarity): Wertheimer listed this too, so learned shapes such as letters are grouped. Modern additions: common region (Palmer, 1992: items enclosed in the same boundary group) and uniform connectedness (Palmer and Rock, 1994: physically connected items group most strongly). Figure-ground (Rubin, 1915) is the prior split into object and background, not a grouping law as such.

Why it matters clinically and practically. Closure and good continuation are tested in perceptual tasks such as the Gestalt Completion test (Street, 1931) and Hooper Visual Organization Test (pictures cut into pieces). The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender, 1938) uses nine of Wertheimer's 1923 figures; patients with organic brain damage often fail to reproduce the gestalt correctly.

Key distinctions

LawStem cueExample
ProximityClose together in space or timeDots in pairs seen as pairs
SimilaritySame colour, shape, sizeAlternating filled rows seen as rows
ContinuitySmooth line, same directionX seen as two crossing lines
ClosureFilling gaps, completingBroken circle, Kanizsa triangle
Common fateMoving togetherFlock of birds
PragnanzSimplest, most stable formOlympic rings as five circles
Common regionSame enclosed areaItems inside one box

MCQ traps

  • "Close together in space and time" is proximity, even though "continuity" contains the idea of time flowing; continuity is about direction.
  • Closure vs continuity: closure completes a shape that has gaps; continuity follows a path. A dashed line seen as one line is continuity (and proximity), not closure.
  • Similarity vs common fate: if the stem mentions moving together, it is common fate even if the items also look alike.
  • Pragnanz is the overarching law; if the options include it with "simplest organisation", pick it.
  • Perceptual constancy and depth cues are not Gestalt grouping laws.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q52: Items close together in space and time are perceived as an organised group → Proximity.
