So: optic nerve lesion → blindness in one eye; chiasm lesion (classically a pituitary tumour) → bitemporal hemianopia (tunnel-like loss of both outer fields); optic tract, radiation or occipital cortex lesion → contralateral homonymous hemianopia (same half of the field lost in both eyes), often with macular sparing in occipital stroke; temporal lobe (Meyer's loop) lesion → contralateral superior quadrantanopia ("pie in the sky"); parietal radiation lesion → inferior quadrantanopia. A scotoma is a local blind spot. Blindsight (Weiskrantz, patient D.B.): after V1 damage, patients deny seeing but point to targets above chance.

Agnosias. Freud (1891) coined "agnosia" (not knowing). Lissauer (1890) split visual agnosia into apperceptive (cannot form a whole percept, cannot copy or match shapes) and associative (can copy a drawing perfectly but cannot say what it is). Types: visual object agnosia (objects); prosopagnosia (faces, fusiform gyrus, Bodamer 1947); achromatopsia (colour, V4); akinetopsia (motion, V5/MT; patient L.M., Zihl and colleagues, 1983, saw pouring tea as frozen); simultanagnosia (cannot see more than one object at once, part of Balint's syndrome with optic ataxia and ocular apraxia, bilateral parieto-occipital); topographical agnosia (cannot recognise places); auditory agnosia and amusia; tactile agnosia/astereognosis (parietal); finger agnosia (Gerstmann); anosognosia (Babinski, 1914: unawareness of one's own deficit, e.g. denying a left hemiplegia after right parietal stroke). Oliver Sacks's The Man Who Mistook His Wife for a Hat describes visual agnosia.

Key distinctions

ConditionProblemCorrection / lesion
MyopiaDistant objects blurredConcave lens
HypermetropiaNear objects blurredConvex lens
PresbyopiaNear focus fails with ageConvex reading lens
AstigmatismDistortion at all distancesCylindrical lens
Bitemporal hemianopiaBoth outer fields lostOptic chiasm (pituitary)
Homonymous hemianopiaSame half-field lost in both eyesBehind the chiasm, opposite side
ProsopagnosiaFaces not recognisedFusiform gyrus
AgeusiaLoss of tasteGustatory, not an agnosia

MCQ traps

  • Hyper = far-sighted, sees far well, struggles near. Students flip myopia and hypermetropia; anchor on the British terms: myopia = "short-sighted" (sees only short distances), hypermetropia = "long-sighted".
  • Presbyopia is not hypermetropia. Both need convex lenses, but presbyopia is age-related lens rigidity.
  • Astigmatism is not "distance only". It blurs at every distance.
  • Field defects are neural, not optical. Glasses never fix a hemianopia.
  • Agnosia requires intact sensation. A blind patient who cannot name objects has no agnosia.
  • Prosopagnosia = faces. Taste loss is ageusia; inability to show facial expressions is facial paralysis or hypomimia (e.g. Parkinson's mask), not prosopagnosia.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q67: Difficulty seeing objects at a distance → Myopia; hypermetropia (near blur), astigmatism (blur at all distances from uneven curvature) and visual field defects (pathway lesions, not focusing) were the distractors.
  • Q83: Prosopagnosia results in an inability to → the official key says (b) distinguish tastes, which looks wrong. The textbook answer is (a) recognize faces. "Distinguish tastes" describes ageusia, "recognize objects" (d) is visual object agnosia (a sister agnosia, not face-specific), and "express feelings through facial expressions" (c) is an output problem, not a recognition failure. Write (a) in any descriptive answer.
66Neurodevelopmental disorders (ADHD etc.) vs OCD and BPD
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q18, Q5, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 30 covered autism. This topic is about classification: which DSM-5-TR chapter a disorder belongs to, because Q18 was really a "which chapter" question. DSM-5 (2013) reorganised the manual on a developmental, lifespan logic, and the first chapter became Neurodevelopmental Disorders: conditions with onset in the developmental period, typically before the child enters school, producing deficits in personal, social, academic or occupational functioning. They often co-occur with one another.

The neurodevelopmental chapter contains: (1) intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability, graded mild to profound by adaptive functioning, not IQ alone) and global developmental delay; (2) communication disorders (language disorder, speech sound disorder, childhood-onset fluency disorder or stuttering, social pragmatic communication disorder); (3) autism spectrum disorder; (4) ADHD; (5) specific learning disorder (reading, written expression, mathematics: dyslexia, dyscalculia); (6) motor disorders (developmental coordination disorder, stereotypic movement disorder, and tic disorders including Tourette's). DSM-IV had kept these under "Disorders Usually First Diagnosed in Infancy, Childhood, or Adolescence", a chapter DSM-5 abolished. In India, autism, intellectual disability and specific learning disabilities are among the 21 disabilities recognised by the RPwD Act 2016.

ADHD in depth. A persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity. DSM-5-TR requires 6 or more symptoms in a domain (5 for those aged 17 and older) for at least 6 months, several symptoms present before age 12 (raised from 7 in DSM-IV), present in two or more settings (home, school, work), with clear impairment. Presentations: combined, predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive. DSM-5 also allowed ADHD to be diagnosed alongside autism for the first time. Prevalence is about 7% of children and 2.5% of adults (DSM-5-TR), with boys diagnosed about twice as often in childhood. Russell Barkley models ADHD as a core deficit in behavioural inhibition that disrupts executive functions; neuroimaging shows delayed cortical maturation and frontostriatal dopamine and norepinephrine involvement. Treatment: stimulants (methylphenidate, amphetamines), non-stimulants (atomoxetine), parent training and classroom behaviour management.

Why OCD is not neurodevelopmental. OCD can begin in childhood (and is linked to tics: a "tic-related" specifier exists), but onset is typically in adolescence or early adulthood and it is not a disorder of developmental brain maturation in DSM terms. DSM-5 moved OCD out of the anxiety disorders into its own chapter, Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders, together with body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder, trichotillomania (hair-pulling) and excoriation (skin-picking). It carries an insight specifier (good/fair, poor, absent/delusional).

Why BPD is not neurodevelopmental. Borderline personality disorder is a Cluster B personality disorder (with antisocial, histrionic, narcissistic). Personality disorders are enduring patterns beginning by adolescence or early adulthood. BPD needs 5 of 9 criteria: frantic efforts to avoid abandonment; unstable intense relationships swinging between idealisation and devaluation; identity disturbance; impulsivity in at least two self-damaging areas; recurrent suicidal behaviour or self-harm; affective instability; chronic emptiness; intense inappropriate anger; transient stress-related paranoia or dissociation. Adolf Stern used "borderline" in 1938; Otto Kernberg described borderline personality organisation; Marsha Linehan's biosocial theory and Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT) are the evidence-based treatment. GAD sits in the anxiety disorders chapter.

Key distinctions

DisorderDSM-5-TR chapterTypical onsetOverlap that confuses
ADHDNeurodevelopmentalBefore 12Impulsivity, emotional dysregulation
OCDObsessive-Compulsive and RelatedAdolescence to early adulthood (some childhood)Concentration problems from obsessions
BPDPersonality Disorders (Cluster B)Adolescence / early adulthoodImpulsivity, mood swings
GADAnxiety DisordersAny, median around 30Restlessness, poor concentration
Tourette'sNeurodevelopmental (motor/tic)Before 18High OCD comorbidity

MCQ traps

  • "Childhood onset" does not equal neurodevelopmental. Separation anxiety and childhood OCD start young but sit in other chapters.
  • Tourette's is neurodevelopmental (a motor disorder), even though it travels with OCD.
  • OCD left the anxiety chapter in DSM-5; PTSD also left it (to Trauma- and Stressor-Related).
  • Age cut-offs: ADHD symptoms before 12 (DSM-5) not 7; PD diagnosis under 18 needs features for at least 1 year, and antisocial PD needs age 18.
  • Impulsivity is shared. ADHD impulsivity is developmental and pervasive since childhood; BPD impulsivity is tied to relationship stress and identity instability.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q18: Which disorder is a neurodevelopmental disorder → ADHD; OCD (Obsessive-Compulsive and Related chapter), borderline personality disorder (Cluster B personality disorder) and GAD (anxiety disorders) were the distractors.
  • Q5: Persistent sadness and loss of interest → Depression; OCD sat as a distractor (obsessions and compulsions, not anhedonia), alongside schizophrenia and bipolar disorder.
  • Q78: Defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause (strictly, DSM-5-TR now requires excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours about symptoms rather than "no medical cause"); "obsessive behaviours" was a distractor pointing to OCD.
67Delusions of reference, control, grandiosity
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q36, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 27 listed the delusional themes. Here we go deeper into the three that sat as distractors beside persecution in Q36: reference, control and grandiosity. Recall the base definition (Jaspers, 1913): a delusion is a fixed false belief, held with certainty, incorrigible by evidence, and out of keeping with the person's cultural or religious background.

Ideas vs delusions of reference. In reference, neutral events are experienced as personally meaningful: a newsreader is talking about me, strangers laughing on the street are laughing at me, a song on the radio is a message to me. Psychopathology separates ideas of reference (held with doubt, can be reality-tested, "I know it's silly but I feel they're talking about me", seen in social anxiety, depression and schizotypal personality disorder) from delusions of reference (held with full conviction). DSM-5-TR's schizotypal criteria literally say "ideas of reference (excluding delusions of reference)". Reference is closely related to delusional perception, a Schneiderian first-rank symptom: a normal perception is suddenly given a private, unrelated delusional meaning (the traffic light turned red, so I knew I was chosen to save the world). Reference delusions are common in schizophrenia and in the early "delusional mood" (Wahnstimmung), where everything feels charged with meaning.

Delusions of control (passivity). The belief that one's thoughts, feelings, impulses or actions are being controlled by an outside agency (radio waves, a chip, a god, a neighbour's machine). The core is a loss of ego boundary: experiences that are one's own are felt as alien and imposed. Kurt Schneider grouped these "made" experiences into his first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia: made feelings, made impulses, made volitional acts, somatic passivity, plus thought insertion, thought withdrawal and thought broadcasting, audible thoughts, voices arguing, voices commenting, and delusional perception. Mellor (1970) gave the classic patient examples ("I cry, tears roll down my cheeks and I look unhappy, but inside I have a cold anger because they are using me in this way"). First-rank symptoms are no longer pathognomonic (they also occur in mania and other psychoses), and DSM-5 removed the special weight once given to bizarre delusions, but they remain central in Indian MSE teaching and viva questions.

Grandiose delusions. A fixed belief in inflated worth, power, knowledge, identity or a special relationship with a deity or famous person ("I am the reincarnation of Vivekananda", "I have discovered the cure for cancer"). They are classically mood-congruent in mania (DSM-5-TR "with mood-congruent psychotic features"), also occur in schizophrenia, in delusional disorder, grandiose type, and historically in general paresis of the insane (neurosyphilis). Separate them from grandiosity as a trait: in narcissistic personality disorder the person has a grandiose sense of self-importance but it is not a psychotic, fixed false belief, and in hypomania grandiosity may be inflated self-esteem without delusional conviction.

Delusional disorder (DSM-5-TR): one or more delusions for at least 1 month, functioning not markedly impaired apart from the delusion, and never meeting criterion A for schizophrenia. Subtypes: erotomanic, grandiose, jealous, persecutory, somatic, mixed, unspecified, with a "with bizarre content" specifier.

Key distinctions

ExperienceCore beliefTypical setting
PersecutionI am being harmed, plotted against, discriminated againstSchizophrenia, delusional disorder (commonest)
ReferenceNeutral events are about or aimed at meSchizophrenia; ideas of reference in schizotypal PD, social anxiety
Control / passivityMy thoughts, feelings or actions are made by an outside forceSchizophrenia (first-rank)
GrandioseI am special, powerful, famous, divinely chosenMania, delusional disorder
OCD intrusive thoughtRecognised as a product of my own mindOCD, not a delusion

MCQ traps

  • "Interfered with, plotted against, threatened, discriminated against" = persecution, even though "interfered with" sounds like control. Control is about one's own will being taken over.
  • Reference vs persecution. "The TV is talking about me" is reference; it becomes persecutory only if the content is harm.
  • Ideas vs delusions of reference: conviction decides it. Schizotypal PD has ideas, not delusions.
  • Thought insertion vs obsession. In OCD the person knows the thought is their own (ego-dystonic); in thought insertion it is felt as someone else's.
  • Grandiose delusion vs narcissism. Fixed, false and psychotic vs an inflated self-image without loss of reality testing.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q36: Belief that one is deliberately interfered with, discriminated against, plotted against, threatened or mistreated → Persecution; reference (events about me, not harm), control (external agency running my thoughts or actions) and grandiosity (inflated importance) were all distractors. This topic appeared here only as distractors.
  • Q78: Defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause; "delusions of grandeur" was a distractor because SSD involves distressing bodily preoccupation without psychotic conviction; a fixed false bodily belief would be a somatic delusion, not SSD.
68Panic ("fear of fear"), PTSD flashbacks, OCD checking
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q66, Q93, Q100 · added from the options

From scratch

Q66 asked for GAD (topic 26), but each wrong option was a textbook signature phrase of another disorder: "fear of fear" (panic disorder), "continual flashbacks" (PTSD), "a desire to check that the environment is safe" (OCD checking). Learn the three signatures and the models behind them.

Panic disorder and "fear of fear". A panic attack is an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, with at least 4 of 13 symptoms: palpitations, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, choking, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills or heat, paresthesias, derealisation or depersonalisation, fear of losing control or "going crazy", and fear of dying. Panic disorder (DSM-5-TR) requires recurrent unexpected attacks, with at least one followed by 1 month or more of persistent worry about further attacks or their consequences and/or maladaptive behaviour change (avoiding exercise, carrying a phone everywhere). That anticipatory dread of the next attack is the "fear of fear", a phrase associated with Goldstein and Chambless (1978). David Clark's cognitive model (1986): panic arises from the catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations (racing heart → "I'm having a heart attack" → more fear → more sensations, a vicious circle). Anxiety sensitivity (Reiss and McNally, 1985), the belief that anxiety sensations are dangerous, is the trait vulnerability. Treatment: CBT with interoceptive exposure and SSRIs.

PTSD and flashbacks. DSM-5-TR moved PTSD to Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders. Criterion A: exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence (direct, witnessed, learned of for a close person, or repeated professional exposure to details). Four symptom clusters lasting more than 1 month: intrusion (memories, nightmares, flashbacks, cue-triggered distress), avoidance, negative alterations in cognition and mood (including inability to remember an important aspect of the trauma, persistent blame, detachment), and arousal and reactivity (hypervigilance, exaggerated startle, sleep and concentration problems, irritability). A flashback is a dissociative reaction in which the person feels or acts as if the trauma is happening now, ranging from brief sensory intrusions to complete loss of awareness of present surroundings. That "nowness" separates it from an ordinary intrusive memory, which is known to be past. Models: Brewin's dual representation theory (sensory, image-based memories cut off from contextual memory) and Ehlers and Clark (2000) (a poorly elaborated trauma memory plus negative appraisals produce a sense of current threat). If symptoms last 3 days to 1 month it is acute stress disorder. Treatments: prolonged exposure (Foa), cognitive processing therapy (Resick), trauma-focused CBT, EMDR (Shapiro).

OCD checking. Checking is the commonest compulsion after cleaning. The cycle: an obsession of doubt and harm ("Did I leave the gas on? The house will burn and it will be my fault") → anxiety → compulsion (checking the knob, often in rituals of a set number) → brief relief → the relief negatively reinforces checking (Mowrer's two-factor theory: fear acquired by classical conditioning, maintained by operant avoidance). Salkovskis (1985) and Rachman stressed inflated responsibility: checkers believe they are responsible for preventing harm. Paradoxically, repeated checking reduces memory confidence (van den Hout and Kindt, 2003), so the more one checks, the less sure one feels. Treatment is exposure and response prevention (Victor Meyer, 1966): leave the house without checking, let anxiety rise and fall (habituation), and learn that the feared outcome does not follow (extinction).

Key distinctions

Signature phraseDisorderFocus of fearTime frame
Free-floating apprehension about many thingsGADMany future events6 months
Fear of fearPanic disorderThe next attack, bodily sensations1 month of worry after attacks
Flashbacks, relivingPTSDPast trauma felt as presentMore than 1 month
Checking to prevent harmOCDResponsibility for harmTime-consuming (over 1 h/day) or impairing
Gaps in personal memory after traumaDissociative amnesiaNot fear; memory lossAny

MCQ traps

  • Panic attack is not panic disorder. Attacks occur across many disorders (a specifier in DSM-5-TR); the disorder needs unexpected attacks plus a month of fear of fear.
  • Flashback vs intrusive memory. The flashback is dissociative reliving; an intrusive memory is recognised as a memory.
  • PTSD includes amnesia for parts of the trauma, but when the stem centres on inability to recall important personal information, the answer is dissociative amnesia.
  • ERP works by habituation and extinction, not reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe's systematic desensitisation).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q66: GAD involves → Continued apprehension about unspecified things; "fear of fear" (panic disorder), "continual flashbacks to past events" (PTSD) and "a desire to check that the environment is safe" (OCD checking) were the three distractors, each a signature of another disorder.
  • Q93: Inability to remember important personal information, often following trauma → Dissociative amnesia; PTSD was the near-miss distractor because it can include amnesia for part of the trauma, but its core is intrusion, avoidance and hyperarousal.
  • Q100: ERP is based on which principle → Habituation and extinction; this is the treatment for OCD checking described above, with counter-conditioning and reciprocal inhibition (desensitisation) as distractors.
69Confabulation vs malingering vs factitious disorder
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q68, Q63, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 28 set out the basic grid (conscious or not, internal or external motive). Here we go deeper into the three conditions that MCQs love to swap: confabulation (a memory disorder), factitious disorder (a psychiatric disorder) and malingering (a behaviour, not a disorder).

Confabulation is the production of false or distorted memories about oneself or the world, stated with full conviction and without any intent to deceive. The patient is not lying; he genuinely believes what he says. It was described in Korsakoff's syndrome (Sergei Korsakoff, 1887), where thiamine deficiency damages the mammillary bodies and dorsomedial thalamus. Kopelman (1987) split it into two kinds: provoked (momentary) confabulation, plausible errors produced only when questioned ("Yesterday I went to the market"), common in many amnesias; and spontaneous (fantastic) confabulation, unprompted and often bizarre, sometimes acted upon. Spontaneous confabulation is tied to damage of the ventromedial and orbitofrontal prefrontal cortex and basal forebrain, classically after rupture of an anterior communicating artery aneurysm. The underlying problem is a failure of source and temporal monitoring: old memories are retrieved but not tagged as old, so they are reported as current. Related phenomena: Anton syndrome (cortical blindness with denial and confabulated descriptions of what the patient "sees"), anosognosia after right parietal stroke, and Gazzaniga's split-brain "interpreter", where the left hemisphere invents a reason for actions it did not initiate.

Factitious disorder (DSM-5-TR, Somatic Symptom and Related Disorders) has two forms. Imposed on self: (A) falsification of physical or psychological signs or symptoms, or induction of injury or disease, associated with identified deception; (B) the person presents himself as ill, impaired or injured; (C) the deceptive behaviour is evident even in the absence of obvious external rewards; (D) not better explained by another disorder such as delusional disorder. Imposed on another: the same, but the victim is another person (usually a child); the perpetrator, not the victim, receives the diagnosis. Methods include injecting saliva or faeces, taking insulin or warfarin, heating thermometers, contaminating urine with blood. The severe chronic form, Munchausen syndrome (Richard Asher, 1951), features wandering between hospitals, dramatic but vague histories, pseudologia fantastica (elaborate pathological lying), many surgical scars, eagerness for invasive procedures, and discharge against advice when confronted. Motive: the psychological need to occupy the sick role.

Malingering is the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated symptoms motivated by external incentives: compensation, avoiding military duty or work, evading prosecution, obtaining drugs. DSM-5-TR codes it among "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention" (Z76.5), not as a mental disorder. Suspicion should arise with a medicolegal context, marked discrepancy between claimed distress and objective findings, poor cooperation, and antisocial personality disorder. Detection uses symptom validity tests: the Test of Memory Malingering (TOMM, Tombaugh 1996), Rey's 15-Item Test, forced-choice tests where scoring below chance implies knowing the right answer, MMPI-2 over-reporting scales (F, Fb, Fp, FBS), and the Structured Interview of Reported Symptoms (SIRS, Rogers).

Ganser syndrome sits in between: approximate answers (vorbeireden, "2 + 2 = 5", "a horse has 5 legs"), clouding of consciousness, pseudohallucinations, first described in prisoners (Ganser, 1898); now classed as other specified dissociative disorder.

Key distinctions

FeatureConfabulationFactitious disorderMalingering
Awareness of falsityNone, sincereConscious productionConscious production
MotiveNone (brain dysfunction)Internal: sick roleExternal gain
StatusNeuropsychological signDSM mental disorderZ-code, not a disorder
Typical settingKorsakoff, ACoA aneurysm, frontal injuryHospitals, health-care workersCourt, insurance, army, prison
Ends whenMonitoring recovers, or neverRarely, even when exposedOnce the incentive is gone

MCQ traps

  • "Fabrication of memories" without the word "deliberate" = confabulation; it is a memory sign, not deception.
  • "Consciously faking ... for a nonmedical objective" = malingering. "Nonmedical" is the giveaway that the goal is external.
  • In factitious disorder imposed on another, the diagnosis goes to the caregiver who fakes the child's illness.
  • Below-chance scores on a two-choice memory test suggest malingering, not severe amnesia.
  • Ganser's approximate answers are not confabulation; the patient gives near-miss answers to simple questions.
  • Pseudologia fantastica (lying about life history) belongs to factitious disorder, not to Korsakoff.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q68: consciously faking illness to achieve a specific nonmedical objective → Malingering. Confabulation sat as a distractor and was wrong because it is unintentional memory filling; "altered identities" (DID) and "false identification" (misidentification) were also distractors.
  • Q63: severe memory impairment and confusion from chronic alcohol use → Korsakoff's syndrome; confabulation does not appear as an option but is the hallmark sign named in the explanation.
  • Q78: defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause; malingering and factitious disorder appear only in the explanation as the consciously produced conditions SSD must be separated from.
70Derealization vs depersonalization, dissociative fugue
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q88, Q93, Q69 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 29 gave the definitions. This topic adds phenomenology, history, causes and the differential diagnosis that NIMHANS-style viva and MCQs probe.

Depersonalization is an altered experience of the self: feeling detached from one's own mind or body, as if an outside observer. Patients say "I feel like a robot", "my voice does not sound like mine", "my hands look like someone else's", "I have no feelings, I am numb", "I am watching myself in a film". Five components are usually described: anomalous body experience, emotional numbing, anomalous subjective recall (memories feel as if they did not happen to me), alienation from surroundings, and a sense of unreality of the self. Derealization is an altered experience of the world: surroundings look flat, two-dimensional, foggy, dreamlike, artificial, "like a stage set" or "behind glass", with distortions of size, distance and time. Both are described in "as if" language. The term depersonalization was introduced by the French psychiatrist Ludovic Dugas (1898); the Cambridge group (Sierra and Berrios) built the Cambridge Depersonalization Scale (2000).

Depersonalization/derealization disorder (DSM-5-TR): (A) persistent or recurrent depersonalization, derealization or both; (B) reality testing remains intact; (C) clinically significant distress or impairment; (D) not due to a substance (cannabis, hallucinogens, ketamine) or medical condition (temporal lobe epilepsy, migraine, head injury); (E) not better explained by another disorder (schizophrenia, panic disorder, depression, PTSD, another dissociative disorder). DSM-5 merged the two experiences into one disorder because they co-occur so often. Lifetime transient experiences are very common; the disorder has a prevalence of roughly 2 percent, with a mean onset in mid-adolescence and equal sex ratio. Triggers include severe stress, emotional abuse in childhood, panic attacks and drug use. Treatment is mainly CBT (reattributing the symptoms, reducing avoidance and checking); no drug is clearly established.

Dissociative fugue (Latin fuga, flight) is sudden, unexpected travel away from home or purposeful-seeming wandering, with amnesia for one's identity or past, sometimes with assumption of a partial or wholly new identity. During the fugue the person appears normal to strangers. Recovery is often abrupt, after which the person is confused about where he is and has no memory of the fugue period. Historically, the French "mad traveller" epidemic began with Albert Dadas, described by Philippe Tissie (1887). DSM-IV listed fugue as a separate disorder; DSM-5 made it a specifier: dissociative amnesia with dissociative fugue. Precipitants: war, disasters, marital or financial crisis, legal threat. Differential: complex partial (temporal lobe) seizures with postictal automatism (brief, no complex goal-directed travel, EEG changes), alcoholic blackouts, transient global amnesia (identity preserved, repetitive questioning, lasts hours), and malingering when there is legal trouble.

Key distinctions

TermWhat feels alteredReality testingMemory
DepersonalizationSelf, body, own feelingsIntactIntact
DerealizationExternal worldIntactIntact
Nihilistic (Cotard) delusion"I am dead, my organs are rotting"Lost (psychotic)Intact
Dissociative fugueIdentity, with travelIntactAmnesia for identity and for the fugue
Transient global amnesiaNew learningIntactAnterograde; identity kept
  • "As if I am unreal" = depersonalization; "I am unreal" held as fact = delusion.
  • Jamais vu (familiar feels new) and deja vu are related temporal-lobe experiences, not the same as derealization.

MCQ traps

  • The word "unreality" alone does not decide; ask unreality of what. Self → depersonalization, surroundings → derealization.
  • Insight is preserved in DPDR; an option saying "loss of reality testing" is wrong.
  • Fugue requires travel or wandering; amnesia for identity without travel is generalized dissociative amnesia.
  • In DSM-5-TR fugue is a specifier, not a separate diagnosis; a question asking for the "DSM-5 disorder" will not list fugue on its own.
  • DID needs two or more personality states; a fugue's new identity is usually single and replaces the old one.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q88: profound disconnection or estrangement from oneself, unreality of one's own thoughts, feelings or identity → Depersonalization. Derealization was the closest distractor (unreality of the surroundings, not the self); fugue state was wrong because nothing in the stem mentions travel or loss of identity; dissociative amnesia was wrong because memory is intact.
  • Q93: inability to remember important personal information after trauma → Dissociative amnesia; fugue is not offered, but it is the travel specifier of this very disorder.
  • Q69: the DID identity carrying the person's real name → Host identity; useful contrast because a fugue's assumed identity is not an "alter".
71Defences: rationalization, displacement, sublimation
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q30, Q4 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 31 introduced id, ego, superego and the main defences. These three appeared as wrong options next to projection, so here each gets the depth an examiner can test: origin, mechanism, subtypes and classic examples.

Rationalization. The term was introduced into psychoanalysis by Ernest Jones (1908). The ego supplies a logical, socially acceptable explanation for behaviour whose real motive is unacceptable or unconscious. It differs from lying because the person believes the explanation. Two folk forms: sour grapes (from Aesop: the fox decides the unreachable grapes were sour anyway; "I did not really want that seat at NIMHANS") and sweet lemon (insisting that an unwanted outcome is actually good: "failing was the best thing that happened to me"). Vaillant places it among the neurotic (intermediate) defences. Rationalization is the psychoanalytic cousin of Festinger's dissonance reduction: both produce justifications after the act. Janis listed collective rationalization as a symptom of groupthink, where the group explains away warnings.

Displacement. The drive and the feeling stay the same, but the target changes from a dangerous object to a safer one. Freud first described it in The Interpretation of Dreams (1900) as part of the dream-work (with condensation, symbolisation and secondary revision): emotional intensity is shifted from an important dream thought onto a trivial detail. In phobias, anxiety about one object is displaced onto another: in the Little Hans case (1909) a boy's fear of his father (castration anxiety) was displaced onto horses. In everyday life it is the "kick the dog" chain. In social psychology the same idea underlies scapegoating: the frustration-aggression hypothesis (Dollard, Doob, Miller, Mowrer and Sears, 1939) says frustration produces aggression, which is often displaced onto weak, available targets such as minorities. Turning against the self (anger at others redirected onto oneself) is a special case linked by Freud to melancholia.

Sublimation. An unacceptable sexual or aggressive impulse is channelled into a socially valued, productive activity, and the impulse is partly gratified rather than blocked. Freud regarded it as the basis of art, science and civilisation; in his essay on Leonardo da Vinci (1910) he argued that Leonardo's sexual curiosity was sublimated into scientific curiosity. Examples: aggression into surgery, boxing, debating or policing; voyeuristic curiosity into photography or research. Anna Freud added it to the list as the most adaptive defence. In Vaillant's hierarchy (Adaptation to Life, 1977, from the Harvard Grant Study) sublimation is mature, with altruism, humour, suppression and anticipation, and men who used mature defences had better health, marriages and careers in later life. DSM-IV's Defensive Functioning Scale called this the "high adaptive level".

All three are unconscious, all reduce anxiety, and they can co-occur: a man humiliated by his boss (impulse: anger) may yell at his son (displacement), explain that the boy needed discipline (rationalization), or take up competitive sport (sublimation).

Key distinctions

DefenceWhat changesVaillant levelSignature clue
ProjectionOwner of the impulse ("he hates me")ImmatureAttributes
DisplacementTarget of the impulseNeuroticSafer substitute object
SublimationForm of the impulse, into valued activityMatureSocially useful outlet
RationalizationThe explanation for behaviourNeuroticPlausible false reason
IntellectualizationEmotion stripped, abstract talkNeuroticCold, technical language

MCQ traps

  • Displacement vs sublimation: if the substitute outlet is merely safer (the dog, the sibling), it is displacement; if it is admired and productive (art, surgery), it is sublimation.
  • Rationalization vs intellectualization: rationalization gives excuses; intellectualization removes feeling by theorising.
  • Rationalization is not conscious lying; deliberate excuse-making is not a defence.
  • Phobia in classic psychoanalysis is explained by displacement (Little Hans), not projection.
  • Sublimation is the only classic Freudian defence regarded as fully healthy; do not label it immature.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q30: unconsciously attributing one's own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else → Projection. Rationalization (option a), displacement (option c) and sublimation (option d) were all distractors: rationalization gives a false reason, displacement keeps the feeling as one's own but moves its target, and sublimation turns it into a valued activity. None of them attributes the feeling to another person.
  • Q4: groupthink → collective decision-making that discourages dissent; rationalization appears only in the explanation, as Janis's symptom of collective rationalization.
72Maslow, Skinner's radical behaviourism, early vs later social learning
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q7, Q20, Q12, Q73, Q92, Q58 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 33 solved the Q7 match. This topic teaches each theorist in the list properly, so that any rearranged version of the match can be solved from understanding.

Abraham Maslow, "A Theory of Human Motivation" (1943) and Motivation and Personality (1954), a founder of humanistic psychology (the "third force" after psychoanalysis and behaviourism). His hierarchy of needs: physiological → safety → belongingness and love → esteem → self-actualization. The lower four are deficiency (D) needs: their lack creates tension, and once satisfied they stop motivating. Self-actualization is a growth or being (B) need, which grows stronger as it is fed. Lower needs are prepotent and must be largely, not completely, satisfied before higher ones dominate. Maslow studied people he judged self-actualized (Lincoln, Jefferson, Einstein, Eleanor Roosevelt) and listed traits: accurate perception of reality, acceptance of self and others, spontaneity, problem-centring, autonomy, need for privacy, fresh appreciation, peak experiences, deep but few relationships, democratic character, unhostile humour, creativity, resistance to enculturation. Later he added self-transcendence above self-actualization. Criticism: little empirical support for a strict order (Wahba and Bridwell, 1976) and a biased, small sample.

B. F. Skinner: The Behavior of Organisms (1938), Walden Two (1948), Science and Human Behavior (1953), Verbal Behavior (1957), Beyond Freedom and Dignity (1971), About Behaviorism (1974). Radical behaviourism is radical because it does not exclude private events: thoughts and feelings are behaviour inside the skin and obey the same laws, but they are never causes; causes lie in the environment and reinforcement history. This differs from Watson's methodological behaviourism, which simply refused to study anything not publicly observable. Skinner rejected inner agents (will, ego, mind) as "explanatory fictions". His method was the experimental analysis of behaviour: single subjects, cumulative records, schedules of reinforcement. Chomsky's 1959 review of Verbal Behavior is often cited as a trigger of the cognitive revolution.

Early social learning: John Dollard and Neal Miller, Social Learning and Imitation (1941) and Personality and Psychotherapy (1950). At Yale they translated Freudian concepts into Hull's drive-reduction learning theory. Four fundamentals of learning: drive, cue, response, reward. Imitation itself is a learned response that is reinforced (matched-dependent behaviour). Neurosis is learned conflict; Miller's approach-avoidance gradients (avoidance gradient steeper than approach) explain hesitation near the goal. They also co-authored Frustration and Aggression (1939).

Later social learning: Albert Bandura and Richard Walters, Adolescent Aggression (1959) and Social Learning and Personality Development (1963). They showed that learning happens through observation alone, without the learner being reinforced (Bobo doll studies, 1961 to 1965), and distinguished acquisition from performance; vicarious reinforcement affects performance. Bandura later wrote Social Learning Theory (1977) with four processes (attention, retention, motor reproduction, motivation), introduced self-efficacy (1977) and reciprocal determinism (person, behaviour, environment), and renamed the approach social cognitive theory (1986). Julian Rotter (1954) built a parallel cognitive social learning theory around expectancy and reinforcement value.

Key distinctions

TheoristLabelIs reinforcement necessary for learning?Role of cognition
WatsonMethodological behaviourismClassical conditioning focusExcluded from study
SkinnerRadical behaviourismYes, consequences shape behaviourPrivate events are behaviour, not causes
Dollard and MillerEarly social learningYes, imitation is rewardedMinimal, Hullian drives
Bandura and WaltersLater social learningNo, observation sufficesCentral (attention, memory, expectancy)
MaslowSelf-actualizationNot a learning theoryGrowth motivation

MCQ traps

  • "Radical" does not mean "rejects the mind entirely"; that is closer to Watson. Skinner accepted private events but denied them causal status.
  • Dollard and Miller are early (1941), not later; the dividing line is whether imitation needs direct reward.
  • Self-actualization is shared by Maslow and Rogers (and Goldstein, who coined it), but in a list with Skinner and Bandura it goes to Maslow.
  • Reciprocal determinism (Bandura) is not reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe).
  • Social learning theory is Bandura, not Festinger; operant conditioning is Skinner, not Bandura.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q7: match Dollard and Miller, Skinner, Bandura and Walters, Maslow → A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2. The wrong combinations swap early and later social learning or give self-actualization to Skinner.
  • Q20: Bobo doll and observational learning → Bandura; Skinner was the distractor because his learning needs the learner's own reinforcement.
  • Q12: Festinger → cognitive dissonance; social learning theory and operant conditioning were distractors.
  • Q73: stream of consciousness → William James; Skinner was a distractor.
  • Q92 and Q58: Skinner and social learning appear only in the explanations (Chomsky's critique of Skinner; Rotter's social learning theory behind locus of control).
73Transference (positive / negative) vs countertransference
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q99 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 35 gave the four-cell grid (whose feelings, which direction). This topic covers where transference came from, how it is used in therapy, and the finer countertransference vocabulary.

Origin. Breuer's patient Anna O. (Bertha Pappenheim, 1880 to 1882) developed intense feelings toward Breuer, who withdrew from the case. Freud named the phenomenon in Studies on Hysteria (1895) and first understood its force in the Dora case (1905), where he later concluded that the treatment broke off because he had failed to notice and interpret Dora's transference of feelings about Herr K onto him. Transference is the unconscious repetition, in the relationship with the therapist, of feelings, wishes and patterns originally attached to important figures of childhood. It is a repetition instead of remembering.

Positive transference (affection, trust, admiration, idealisation) initially helps by building cooperation; Freud separated "unobjectionable" positive transference (the basis of the alliance) from erotic transference, which he discussed in "Observations on Transference-Love" (1915), insisting the analyst must neither gratify nor reject it but analyse it. Negative transference (hostility, suspicion, contempt) often shows as lateness, missed sessions, arguing, and is a major source of resistance. Within analysis the patient's original conflicts become organised around the analyst, a transference neurosis, which is then interpreted and worked through. Transference is fostered by the analyst's neutrality and abstinence (the "blank screen").

Working alliance. Greenson (1965) distinguished the transference from the working alliance, the realistic collaboration between the patient's adult ego and the therapist; Bordin (1979) defined the alliance as agreement on goals, tasks and bond, and it is one of the best predictors of outcome across all therapies. Luborsky's Core Conflictual Relationship Theme (CCRT: wish, response of other, response of self) operationalised transference for research.

Countertransference. Freud (1910) used the term for the analyst's unconscious reactions to the patient arising from the analyst's own unresolved conflicts, and recommended the analyst's personal analysis to control it. This is the classical (narrow) view. The totalistic (broad) view, from Paula Heimann (1950), treats all of the therapist's emotional responses as a useful instrument for understanding the patient. Heinrich Racker separated concordant countertransference (the therapist identifies with the patient's own feelings) from complementary countertransference (the therapist feels like the patient's internal other, e.g. the critical parent). Winnicott's "Hate in the Counter-Transference" (1949) argued that objective hatred of some patients is real and must be acknowledged. Klein's concept of projective identification explains how a patient can induce feelings in the therapist.

Negative countertransference = therapist's irritation, boredom, dislike, contempt, wish to end the case. Positive countertransference = over-involvement, rescue fantasies, extending sessions, boundary slips. Both are managed by self-awareness, supervision and personal therapy. In CBT, Beck and later Safran and Segal discussed the therapeutic relationship and interpersonal schemas; in client-centred therapy Rogers rejected transference interpretation altogether.

Key distinctions

ConceptSourceExample
Positive transferencePatient"You are the only one who understands me"
Negative transferencePatient"You are just like my father, you don't care"
Positive countertransferenceTherapistWants to rescue, extends sessions
Negative countertransferenceTherapistDreads sessions, feels irritated
Concordant vs complementaryTherapist (Racker)Feels the patient's feeling vs feels like the patient's significant other
Working allianceBoth, realisticAgreement on goals and tasks

MCQ traps

  • "Negative feelings" in the stem pulls you to negative transference; check whose feelings first.
  • When both "countertransference" and "negative countertransference" are offered and the stem specifies negative feelings of the therapist, the more specific option is the key.
  • Resistance is not transference; resistance is any opposition to the work of therapy, and negative transference is one source of it.
  • The working alliance is the realistic part of the relationship, not a form of transference.
  • Countertransference was first seen as an obstacle (Freud), later as a tool (Heimann); both answers can appear, so read the theorist in the stem.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q99: the process in which the therapist expresses negative feelings toward patients → Negative counter transference. Negative transference was the main distractor and was wrong because it describes the patient's hostility toward the therapist; positive transference was wrong on both counts (patient, warm); plain counter transference was correct in direction but less specific than option d.
74Wolpe's reciprocal inhibition, counterconditioning, systematic desensitization vs ERP, Bateson's double bind
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q100, Q98, Q20 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 36 covered ERP and topic 35 the basics of the double bind. Here we go deeper into Wolpe's work, the logic of counterconditioning, the step-by-step method of systematic desensitization, and the formal structure of the double bind.

Counterconditioning before Wolpe. Mary Cover Jones (1924), working under Watson's guidance, treated "Peter", a boy afraid of rabbits, by bringing the rabbit gradually closer while Peter ate food he liked. The pleasant response displaced the fear: counterconditioning is replacing a conditioned response with a new, incompatible one to the same stimulus. Jones is often called the mother of behaviour therapy.

Joseph Wolpe (South African psychiatrist) produced "experimental neurosis" in cats by shocking them in a cage; the cats then refused to eat there. He found they could be gradually fed in rooms less and less like the cage until the fear was gone, and concluded that eating inhibited anxiety. He borrowed Sherrington's neurophysiological term and stated the principle of reciprocal inhibition: if a response antagonistic to anxiety is made in the presence of anxiety-evoking stimuli so that it is accompanied by a complete or partial suppression of anxiety, the bond between those stimuli and anxiety is weakened. The book is Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (1958). Anxiety-antagonistic responses he used: deep muscle relaxation (Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation, 1938), assertive responses (for social anxiety, the origin of assertiveness training) and sexual responses (for sexual anxieties).

Systematic desensitization (SD) has three steps: (1) relaxation training, usually over several sessions; (2) building an anxiety hierarchy of about 10 to 15 scenes ranked by SUDS (subjective units of disturbance, 0 to 100, Wolpe's scale); (3) pairing: while deeply relaxed, the client imagines the lowest scene; if anxiety appears he signals, stops and relaxes again; only when a scene produces no anxiety does he move up. SD is usually imaginal, gradual and anxiety is kept low. Lang and Lazovik (1963) showed it worked for snake phobia. Later dismantling research suggested that relaxation is not essential and that exposure itself may be the active ingredient, which is why modern therapists explain SD partly through extinction.

SD vs ERP: SD keeps anxiety low and replaces it with a competing response; ERP (Meyer 1966, Foa) deliberately allows anxiety to rise and fall on its own while rituals are blocked, so its principle is habituation and extinction.

Double bind in depth. Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland (1956) listed the necessary ingredients: (1) two or more persons, one the "victim"; (2) repeated experience, so the bind becomes an expected pattern; (3) a primary negative injunction ("do not do that or I will punish you"); (4) a secondary injunction conflicting with the first at a more abstract level, often non-verbal (tone, posture); (5) a tertiary negative injunction forbidding escape from the field or comment on the contradiction. The idea rested on Russell's theory of logical types: messages and messages about messages (metacommunication) belong to different levels, and the victim cannot metacommunicate. The causal theory of schizophrenia failed, but the concept fed into family systems therapy and the therapeutic double bind (paradoxical injunctions, symptom prescription), used by Milton Erickson and Haley.

Key distinctions

TermWhoCore idea
CounterconditioningMary Cover Jones (1924)Replace the CR with an incompatible response
Reciprocal inhibitionWolpe (1958)Two incompatible responses cannot co-occur; the stronger suppresses the weaker
Systematic desensitizationWolpeRelaxation + graded imagined hierarchy
ERPMeyer, FoaExposure without escape; habituation and extinction
Reciprocal determinismBanduraPerson, behaviour, environment influence each other
Double bindBateson et al. (1956)Contradictory messages at two levels, no escape

MCQ traps

  • Reciprocal inhibition and counterconditioning are near-synonyms for SD; neither explains ERP.
  • "Graded exposure" describes pacing; SD and ERP can both be graded.
  • Wolpe, not Jacobson, created SD; Jacobson created progressive muscle relaxation.
  • Reciprocal determinism (Bandura) is a frequent look-alike for reciprocal inhibition.
  • A double bind requires the inability to comment or escape; simple mixed messages or "faulty communication" are not enough.
  • Bateson is an anthropologist, not a psychiatrist; Fromm-Reichmann gave the schizophrenogenic mother.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q100: ERP is based on → Habituation and extinction. Counter conditioning (a) and reciprocal inhibition (c) were distractors: they are Wolpe's principles for systematic desensitization and need a competing response such as relaxation, which ERP does not use. Graded exposure (d) is a procedure, not a principle.
  • Q98: one parent conveys two conflicting messages at the same time → Double bind communication; "faulty communication" was the vague distractor.
  • Q20: Bobo doll → Bandura; reciprocal determinism appears only in the explanation and should not be confused with Wolpe's reciprocal inhibition.
75MMPI, TAT, MBTI
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q17 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 38 dealt with the Rorschach and the projective family. The other three options of Q17 are major tests in their own right and are asked independently, so here is each in depth.

MMPI (Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory). Developed by psychologist Starke Hathaway and psychiatrist J. C. McKinley at the University of Minnesota, published 1943. It is the most widely used and researched objective personality test. Its method was empirical criterion keying: items were kept not because they looked relevant but because they statistically separated a criterion group (for example, diagnosed depressed patients) from normal controls, mostly visitors to the hospital. The original had 550 true/false items (566 with repeats). Ten clinical scales: 1 Hypochondriasis (Hs), 2 Depression (D), 3 Hysteria (Hy), 4 Psychopathic deviate (Pd), 5 Masculinity-femininity (Mf), 6 Paranoia (Pa), 7 Psychasthenia (Pt), 8 Schizophrenia (Sc), 9 Hypomania (Ma), 0 Social introversion (Si). Validity scales: ? (Cannot Say, omitted items), L (Lie, naive attempts to look good), F (Infrequency, faking bad or random responding), K (Correction, subtle defensiveness, used to correct some clinical scales). Scores are T-scores (mean 50, SD 10). Profiles are read as code types from the two highest scales, e.g. 2-7 (anxious depression), 4-9 (acting out), and the 1-3 "conversion V". Revisions: MMPI-2 (Butcher and colleagues, 1989, 567 items, clinical cut-off T 65, new validity scales VRIN, TRIN, F(b), F(p) and the Fake Bad Scale), MMPI-A for adolescents (1992), MMPI-2-RF (2008, 338 items, Restructured Clinical scales), and MMPI-3 (2020, 335 items). Limitations: long, US-normed, culturally loaded items.

TAT (Thematic Apperception Test). Christiana Morgan and Henry Murray (1935), Harvard Psychological Clinic, elaborated in Murray's Explorations in Personality (1938). It rests on Murray's need-press theory: behaviour results from internal needs (achievement, affiliation, dominance, aggression and so on) interacting with environmental press. The set has 31 cards (30 pictures and 1 blank card, 16), coded for boys, girls, males and females; Murray gave 20 cards in two sessions, but most clinicians now use a selection of 8 to 12. The person tells a story for each picture: what led up to it, what is happening, what the characters think and feel, and how it ends. Interpretation looks at the hero (with whom the teller identifies), needs, press, themes (thema) and outcomes. It is a construction type projective (Lindzey). McClelland and Atkinson used TAT-type pictures to score need for achievement (n Ach). Children's version: Bellak's CAT (1949, animal figures). An Indian adaptation was prepared by Uma Chowdhury.

MBTI (Myers-Briggs Type Indicator). Developed by Katharine Cook Briggs and her daughter Isabel Briggs Myers from the 1940s, based on Jung's Psychological Types (1921). Four dichotomies: Extraversion-Introversion (E/I), Sensing-Intuition (S/N), Thinking-Feeling (T/F), and Judging-Perceiving (J/P), the last added by Briggs and Myers. Combinations give 16 types, e.g. INFP. It is a forced-choice self-report, used in counselling, careers and organisations, and is not a clinical instrument. Criticisms: it forces continuous traits into types, many people get a different type on retest, and it has weak predictive validity. McCrae and Costa (1989) showed its scales map onto four of the Big Five (no neuroticism scale).

Key distinctions

TestTypeBasisOutputUse
MMPIObjective, true/falseEmpirical keyingClinical + validity scale profilePsychopathology, forensic
TATProjective, constructionMurray's need-pressThemes, needs, heroPersonality dynamics
MBTIObjective, forced choiceJung's types16 typesNon-clinical, careers
RorschachProjective, associationPerception of inkblotsLocation, determinants, contentThought, reality testing
16PFObjectiveCattell's factor analysis16 source traitsNormal personality

MCQ traps

  • MBTI has 16 types; Cattell's 16PF has 16 factors. Do not swap them.
  • MMPI was built empirically, not rationally or by factor analysis; this is its signature.
  • F scale = faking bad, L scale = naive faking good, K = subtle defensiveness.
  • Scale 7 Psychasthenia corresponds to today's anxiety and OCD, not psychosis.
  • TAT author pair is Morgan and Murray; CAT is Bellak; Rorschach is Hermann Rorschach.
  • MBTI derives from Jung, not Freud; J/P was not in Jung's original scheme.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q17: personality assessment tool that uses inkblots to assess thought processes → Rorschach Test. All three topics here were distractors: MMPI (objective true/false inventory of psychopathology), TAT (projective, but with pictures and stories, not inkblots), and MBTI (objective Jungian type indicator, non-clinical).
76Types of validity, generalizability, sensitivity
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q75, Q24, Q29, Q60 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 37 covered reliability and the test-validity family (face, content, criterion, construct). Here we add the research-design validities, generalizability theory and sensitivity/specificity, the three areas that were only named before.

Validity in research designs. Campbell and Stanley (1963) separated internal validity (did the independent variable really cause the change?) from external validity (do the results generalise to other people, settings, times?). Cook and Campbell (1979) added statistical conclusion validity (is the covariation real, given power and error rates?) and construct validity of cause and effect (do the manipulations and measures represent the intended constructs?). Campbell and Stanley's threats to internal validity are a favourite list: history (outside events during the study), maturation (natural change in participants), testing (pretest affects posttest), instrumentation (change in measuring tool or raters), statistical regression (extreme scorers move toward the mean), selection (groups differ at the start), experimental mortality (differential dropout) and selection-maturation interaction. Randomization controls most of these. Threats to external validity include reactive effects of testing, interaction of selection with treatment, and reactive arrangements (Hawthorne effect). Ecological validity is whether findings hold in natural, real-life settings, often traded off against tight laboratory control. Two more test terms: incremental validity (does a test add prediction beyond existing information?) and cross-validation (checking a validity coefficient on a new sample, where it usually shrinks).

Generalizability theory (G theory). Cronbach, Gleser, Nanda and Rajaratnam (1972). Classical test theory has one undifferentiated error term; G theory uses analysis of variance to split error into facets such as raters, items, occasions and settings. A person's universe score is the average over all admissible conditions (the analogue of the true score). A G study estimates the variance components; a D study (decision study) uses them to plan, e.g. how many raters and items are needed for adequate dependability. It yields a generalizability coefficient (for relative, rank-order decisions) and a dependability (phi) coefficient (for absolute decisions). It is a reliability framework, not a type of validity, even though its name sounds like external validity.

Sensitivity and specificity describe a test's diagnostic accuracy against a gold standard, using a 2 x 2 table. Sensitivity = true positives / (true positives + false negatives): the proportion of people who have the disorder whom the test detects. Specificity = true negatives / (true negatives + false positives): the proportion without the disorder whom the test correctly clears. Positive predictive value (of those testing positive, how many really have it) and negative predictive value depend on the base rate (prevalence); sensitivity and specificity do not. Lowering the cut-off raises sensitivity and lowers specificity. The ROC curve plots sensitivity against 1 minus specificity for each cut-off; the area under the curve (AUC) summarises accuracy (0.5 = chance, 1.0 = perfect). Mnemonics: SnNout (a highly sensitive test, when negative, rules out) and SpPin (a highly specific test, when positive, rules in). Screening tools, such as the GHQ-12 or PHQ-9, are chosen for high sensitivity; confirmatory tests for high specificity. Clinicians also speak of sensitivity to change (responsiveness): whether a scale detects real improvement after treatment.

Key distinctions

TermQuestion answeredFamily
Internal validityDid X cause Y in this study?Research design
External validityDoes it hold elsewhere?Research design
Ecological validityDoes it hold in real life?Research design
Construct validity (test)Does the test measure the theoretical trait?Psychometric
Generalizability coefficientHow dependable across raters, items, occasions?Reliability (G theory)
SensitivityDoes it catch true cases?Diagnostic accuracy
SpecificityDoes it clear true non-cases?Diagnostic accuracy

MCQ traps

  • Random assignment protects internal validity; random sampling protects external validity.
  • Regression to the mean is a threat to internal validity, common when participants are chosen for extreme scores.
  • G theory is an extension of reliability, not validity; do not pick it for "accuracy".
  • PPV falls when prevalence falls even if sensitivity and specificity stay the same.
  • Sensitivity is about the sick (true positive rate); specificity is about the healthy (true negative rate). Students reverse them.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q75: the term for consistency of a measure → Reliability. Validity (accuracy, not consistency), generalizability (G theory is a broader reliability framework, and the plain term asked for is reliability) and sensitivity (true positive rate, a diagnostic accuracy index) were all distractors.
  • Q24: assigning subjects to treatment groups with equal probability → Randomization, the safeguard of internal validity; random sampling, the distractor, serves external validity.
  • Q29: confounding variable → a variable that influences both the IV and DV, a threat to internal validity.
  • Q60: best design for cause and effect → Experimental design, because manipulation plus random assignment gives high internal validity.
77Rater errors: similarity, central tendency, contrast, leniency
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q54, Q15 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 39 covered the halo error. This topic goes deeper on the other three distractors of Q54 (similarity, central tendency, contrast) plus leniency, and on how each is detected statistically and fixed. The classic catalogue of rating errors comes from J. P. Guilford, Psychometric Methods (1936; 2nd ed. 1954), who called them constant errors: systematic biases that push every rating the same way, unlike random error which averages out. Guilford listed the error of leniency, the error of central tendency, the halo effect, the logical error, the contrast error and the proximity error.

Leniency (generosity) error. The rater uses only the top of the scale. Guilford noted raters are especially lenient with people they know well or like, and when ratings carry consequences (appraisals tied to pay, supervisors rating their own trainees). The mirror image is the severity (strictness) error. Statistical signature: the mean rating sits well above (or below) the scale midpoint, and the distribution is negatively skewed (piled up at the high end) for leniency.

Central tendency error. The rater avoids both extremes and marks "average" for nearly everyone. It is common when the rater is unsure, does not know the ratee well, or fears having to justify extreme ratings. Signature: a mean near the midpoint with a very small standard deviation (restricted range). Leniency, severity and central tendency all restrict range, which lowers the correlation of the ratings with any criterion and so lowers validity.

Contrast error. Guilford's original meaning: the rater judges others in the opposite direction from himself on a trait (a very orderly rater sees most others as disorderly). The more common modern meaning is the sequential contrast effect: a candidate is judged against the person rated just before, so an average applicant looks excellent after several weak ones. Selection-interview research from the McGill studies (Webster, 1964) showed interviewers are strongly affected by the quality of preceding candidates.

Similarity (similar-to-me) error. The rater favours people who resemble him or her in background, attitudes, region, college or interests. It is the opposite of Guilford's contrast error (assimilation towards self instead of contrast away from self) and is linked to the similarity-attraction principle in social psychology (Byrne). In clinical settings it shows up when an interviewer warms to a candidate from the same training institute.

Remedies matched to errors. Forced distribution (fixed percentages in each category) counters leniency and central tendency. Forced-choice items (choose between equally desirable statements) counter leniency. Behaviourally Anchored Rating Scales (BARS; Smith and Kendall, 1963) attach concrete behaviours to each scale point. Rating all ratees on one trait before moving to the next counters halo. Randomising the order of candidates and using standardised, structured interviews reduce contrast. Diverse panels and explicit criteria reduce similarity error. Rater training and multiple independent raters help all errors; agreement is checked with Cohen's kappa or the intraclass correlation (ICC).

Key distinctions

ErrorMechanismStatistical signatureBest remedy
Leniency / severityEveryone rated too high / too lowMean far from midpoint, skewed ratingsForced distribution, forced choice
Central tendencyEveryone rated averageTiny SD, restricted rangeForced distribution, BARS
HaloOne trait colours all othersVery high inter-trait correlationsRate one trait across all ratees at a time
ContrastJudged against previous ratee, or opposite to selfRating depends on order of rateesRandomise order, absolute standards
SimilarityPeople like the rater favouredRatings track rater-ratee resemblanceStructured criteria, diverse panels
LogicalTraits believed to go together rated alikeHigh correlation between "logically linked" traitsBehavioural definitions per trait

MCQ traps

  • "Judges on one single attribute" is halo, never similarity. Similarity is about resemblance between rater and ratee, not about one trait spreading.
  • Central tendency error is not "the rater uses the mean"; it is the rater crowding everyone into the middle categories.
  • Contrast vs similarity are opposites: contrast pushes ratings away from the self or from the previous person; similarity pulls ratings towards people like the rater.
  • Leniency is a constant error, not a random one. It raises the mean but need not lower reliability; it mainly harms discrimination and validity.
  • Primacy (first impression) is about order of information within one ratee; contrast is about order across ratees.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q54: Tendency of the rater/interviewer to judge an individual on the basis of one single attribute → Halo error. The three wrong options are exactly this topic: similarity error (favouring ratees who resemble the rater), central tendency error (crowding everyone into the middle) and contrast error (judging against the previous candidate or opposite to oneself). None of them is a single-trait spillover.
  • Q15: What is the halo effect → The influence of a positive trait on the perception of other traits. This topic is not an option here; the distractors are stereotyping, overlooking negatives and primacy, which is why knowing the full error catalogue helps rule them out.
78Child tools: CARS, CBCL, CGAS, ISAA + the 21 disabilities under the RPwD Act
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q85, Q96, Q97 · added from the options

From scratch

Q85 put four child tools side by side. Topic 40 covered ISAA scoring; here are the other three in depth, then the full list of 21 disabilities.

CARS, Childhood Autism Rating Scale (Schopler, Reichler and Renner, 1980, from the TEACCH programme, University of North Carolina). A clinician observes the child and rates 15 items (relating to people, imitation, emotional response, body use, object use, adaptation to change, visual response, listening response, taste/smell/touch response, fear or nervousness, verbal communication, non-verbal communication, activity level, level and consistency of intellectual response, general impressions). Each item is scored 1 to 4 with half points, so totals run 15 to 60. A score of 30 or more suggests autism (30 to 36.5 mild to moderate, 37 to 60 severe). CARS2 (2010) has a Standard version, a High-Functioning version and a parent questionnaire. American, observation-based, autism-specific.

CBCL, Child Behavior Checklist (Thomas Achenbach, part of ASEBA, the Achenbach System of Empirically Based Assessment). A parent-report checklist with forms for ages 1.5 to 5 and 6 to 18; problem items are rated 0, 1 or 2. Factor analysis yields broadband Internalizing (anxious/depressed, withdrawn/depressed, somatic complaints) and Externalizing (rule-breaking, aggressive behaviour) scores, plus social, thought and attention problems. Companions: the Teacher's Report Form (TRF) and Youth Self-Report (YSR, ages 11 to 18). Broad psychopathology screen, not autism-specific, not Indian.

CGAS, Children's Global Assessment Scale (Shaffer and colleagues, 1983), adapted from the adult Global Assessment Scale (Endicott and colleagues, 1976). A single clinician rating from 1 to 100 of a child's overall functioning (ages about 4 to 16); above 70 is the normal range. It measures overall impairment, not any diagnosis.

ISAA (NIMH, Secunderabad, 2009): the Indian scale, 40 items, prescribed for autism severity and disability percentage in certification. Gold-standard research tools elsewhere are the ADOS (observation) and ADI-R (parent interview); M-CHAT is a toddler screen.

The 21 specified disabilities (Schedule, RPwD Act 2016). The 1995 PwD Act had 7 (blindness, low vision, leprosy-cured, hearing impairment, locomotor disability, mental retardation, mental illness). The 2016 Act lists:

GroupDisabilities
Physical: locomotor1 Locomotor disability, 2 Leprosy cured persons, 3 Cerebral palsy, 4 Dwarfism, 5 Muscular dystrophy, 6 Acid attack victims
Physical: sensory and speech7 Blindness, 8 Low vision, 9 Hearing impairment (deaf and hard of hearing), 10 Speech and language disability
Intellectual disability group11 Intellectual disability, 12 Specific learning disabilities, 13 Autism spectrum disorder
Mental behaviour14 Mental illness
Chronic neurological conditions15 Chronic neurological conditions, 16 Multiple sclerosis, 17 Parkinson's disease
Blood disorders18 Haemophilia, 19 Thalassemia, 20 Sickle cell disease
Combined21 Multiple disabilities (including deaf-blindness)

Mnemonic for the 14 additions over 1995: the 3 blood disorders, the 3 neurological (chronic neurological, MS, Parkinson's), the 3 developmental (autism, SLD, cerebral palsy), plus dwarfism, muscular dystrophy, acid attack, speech and language, and multiple disabilities. Benchmark disability = 40% or more.

Key distinctions

ToolAuthor, originFormatMeasures
CARSSchopler et al., USA 198015 items, clinician observation, 15 to 60Autism presence and severity
ISAANIMH, India 200940 items, 6 domains, 40 to 200Autism severity plus % disability for certificate
CBCLAchenbach, USAParent checklist, 0 to 2Internalizing and externalizing problems
CGASShaffer et al., 1983Single rating 1 to 100Global functioning

MCQ traps

  • "Indian tool" + autism = ISAA. CARS is American; ISAA borrowed some CARS content, which is why setters mix them up.
  • Mental illness and intellectual disability are separate entries; autism and SLD sit in the intellectual disability group, not under mental illness.
  • ADHD, dementia and epilepsy are not named entries among the 21; options listing them are distractors.
  • Deaf and hard of hearing count as one disability (hearing impairment); blindness and low vision count as two.
  • IQ below 70 is not an autism criterion; DSM-5-TR requires social communication deficits plus restricted, repetitive behaviours, with intellectual disability recorded as a specifier.
  • CGAS vs CBCL: one number from a clinician vs a long checklist from a parent.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q85: Indian tool to assess severity and disability in children with autism → official key Childhood Autism Rating Scale, but the official key looks wrong: the textbook answer is ISAA, the NIMH scale prescribed for Indian certification. CGAS (global functioning) and CBCL (broad behaviour problems) were distractors because neither is autism-specific or Indian.
  • Q96: Minimum age for SLD assessment under RPwD Act 2016 → 8 years; SLD is one of the 21 disabilities, in the intellectual disability group.
  • Q97: Which is NOT a DSM-5 core autism symptom → IQ below 70; communication, social reciprocity and repetitive behaviour are core domains.
79Normal curve + skewness
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q21 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 41 named the shapes. This topic teaches the normal curve as a measuring tool and how skewness is computed. The normal curve was derived by Abraham de Moivre (1733) as the limit of the binomial, developed by Laplace and Gauss (hence "Gaussian") for errors of observation, applied to human traits by Adolphe Quetelet ("the average man") and to mental ability by Francis Galton (Hereditary Genius, 1869).

Properties. (1) Bell-shaped, unimodal and symmetrical about the mean. (2) Mean = median = mode. (3) Asymptotic: the tails approach but never touch the baseline. (4) Points of inflection at ±1 SD. (5) Total area = 1 (100% of cases). (6) Defined completely by two parameters, mean and SD. (7) Skewness 0, kurtosis mesokurtic.

Area rule (Garrett's table). Mean ± 1 SD holds 68.26% of cases; ± 2 SD holds 95.44%; ± 3 SD holds 99.73%. Between the mean and +1 SD lies 34.13%; between +1 and +2 SD, 13.59%; between +2 and +3 SD, 2.14%. For significance testing: ± 1.96 SD cuts off the middle 95%, ± 2.58 the middle 99%.

Standard scores. Any normal score converts to z = (X minus mean) / SD, giving the standard normal curve (mean 0, SD 1). Derived scales: T score (mean 50, SD 10; MMPI), deviation IQ (mean 100, SD 15; Wechsler), stanine (mean 5, SD about 2, nine bands), sten (mean 5.5, SD 2; 16PF). Worked example: IQ 130 is z = +2, about the 98th percentile; IQ 85 is z = minus 1, about the 16th percentile. An IQ below 70 (z = minus 2) is roughly the bottom 2.3%, which is why it is the intellectual disability cut-off.

Why it matters. Parametric tests (t, ANOVA, Pearson r) assume normally distributed populations. The central limit theorem says sampling distributions of means approach normality as N grows, even when raw scores are not normal. Test constructors deliberately select items of middling difficulty to produce near-normal score distributions.

Skewness is lack of symmetry; the tail names the direction. Positive skew: long tail to the right, most scores bunched low, mean > median > mode (income, reaction times, a very hard test with a floor effect). Negative skew: tail to the left, scores bunched high, mean < median < mode (an easy test with a ceiling effect, age at death in rich countries). The mean is pulled furthest towards the tail; the median sits between mean and mode. Karl Pearson's empirical rule for moderately skewed data: Mode = 3 Median minus 2 Mean.

Measuring it. Pearson's coefficient: Sk = 3(Mean minus Median) / SD (or (Mean minus Mode) / SD). Garrett's percentile measure: Sk = (P90 + P10)/2 minus P50. A positive value means positive skew. Kurtosis (peakedness): leptokurtic (tall, thin), mesokurtic (normal), platykurtic (flat). Garrett's percentile coefficient Ku = Q / (P90 minus P10) equals 0.263 for the normal curve; above 0.263 is platykurtic, below is leptokurtic. Causes of non-normality: selection of a special sample, a test too easy or too hard, biased scoring, or a genuinely skewed trait.

Key distinctions

FeaturePositive skewNegative skew
TailRight (high scores)Left (low scores)
OrderMode < Median < MeanMean < Median < Mode
Test difficultyToo hard (floor)Too easy (ceiling)
Pearson SkPositiveNegative

MCQ traps

  • Skew is named by the tail, not the hump. Scores piled at the high end with a tail to the left is negative skew.
  • Hard test = positive skew, easy test = negative skew. Students reverse this.
  • 68-95-99.7 refers to ± 1, 2, 3 SD; 1.96 (not 2) is the exact 95% cut-off.
  • Kurtosis is not skewness. A leptokurtic curve can be perfectly symmetrical.
  • Garrett's Ku: 0.263 is normal; a larger value means flatter (platykurtic), the opposite of what intuition suggests.
  • A bell curve or skewed distribution describes one variable; it is never a graph of correlational (two-variable) data.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q21: A graphical representation of correlational data is called a → Scatterplot. This topic appeared only as distractors: bell curve and skewed distribution were wrong because both show the shape of a single variable's frequency distribution, while correlation needs two scores per person plotted as points. Chi square was the third distractor (a test statistic, not a graph).
80Random sampling vs randomization vs stratification
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q24 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 42 drew the basic line (sampling = who gets in, randomization = who gets which treatment). Here are the methods behind each word, since setters use all four options of Q24 as named techniques.

Population, sampling frame, sample. The population is everyone you want to generalise to; the sampling frame is the actual list you draw from (a hospital register, voter list); the sample is who you study. Sampling error is the chance difference between a sample statistic and the population parameter; it shrinks as N grows. Sampling bias is systematic and does not shrink with N. Classic warning: the Literary Digest 1936 US election poll drew over two million responses from telephone and car registration lists, over-represented richer voters, and wrongly predicted Landon would beat Roosevelt. Size does not cure a biased frame.

Probability sampling (every member has a known, non-zero chance): (1) Simple random sampling: every member and every combination of members has an equal chance, using the lottery method or a table of random numbers. (2) Systematic sampling: every k-th person from a list after a random start (k = population / sample size). (3) Stratified random sampling: divide the population into homogeneous strata (gender, urban/rural, diagnosis), then randomly sample within each. Proportionate stratification keeps the population proportions; disproportionate oversamples small strata so they can be analysed. It increases precision and guarantees representation of key subgroups. (4) Cluster sampling: randomly select whole groups (schools, villages) and study everyone in them; cheap but less precise. (5) Multistage sampling: clusters, then sub-clusters, then individuals (used in the National Mental Health Survey of India).

Non-probability sampling: convenience (whoever is available, e.g., psychology students), purposive/judgmental (chosen for a characteristic), quota (fill fixed numbers per category, but without random selection, the non-random cousin of stratified sampling), snowball (participants recruit others; hidden populations such as drug users).

Randomization (random assignment) happens after recruitment. R. A.
