Fisher (The Design of Experiments, 1935) argued that only randomization makes groups equivalent on every pre-existing variable, known and unknown, so that differences after treatment can be attributed to the IV. Methods: simple randomization (coin, random numbers), block randomization (keeps group sizes equal within blocks of, say, 4 or 6), stratified randomization (randomize separately within strata such as mild vs severe depression, so severity is balanced across arms), and matched-pairs randomization. In clinical trials, allocation concealment stops recruiters from knowing the next assignment; reporting follows CONSORT.

Randomness is the property of a sequence having no predictable pattern; it is the raw material of both procedures, not a procedure itself. Haphazard choice ("I picked whoever seemed typical") is not random.

Key distinctions

TermQuestion it answersProtects
Random samplingWho from the population enters the study?External validity (generalisation)
RandomizationWhich group does each participant join?Internal validity (causal inference)
StratificationHow do we make sure key subgroups are represented or balanced?Precision, representativeness
RandomnessIs the sequence unpredictable?Underlies both
Stratified vs quotaRandom within strata vs non-random filling of quotasOnly stratified is probability sampling
Stratified vs clusterSample some from every stratum vs take all from some clustersStrata homogeneous inside; clusters heterogeneous inside

MCQ traps

  • "Assignment to treatment groups" in the stem = randomization, even if the words "equal probability" make random sampling look right.
  • A study can have one without the other: an RCT on a convenience sample has randomization but no random sampling (good internal, weaker external validity); a random-sample survey has no randomization.
  • Quota sampling is not stratified random sampling; the absence of random selection is the difference.
  • Bigger sample does not fix sampling bias (Literary Digest).
  • Systematic sampling can be biased if the list has a periodic pattern matching k.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q24: ___ ensures assignment of subjects to treatment groups so every member has equal probability of any assignment → Randomization. All three distractors belong to this topic: random sampling was wrong because it selects people into the study rather than allocating them to groups; randomness is a property of a sequence, not a design procedure; stratification divides a population into subgroups before sampling or randomizing and does not by itself give each person equal probability of every group.
81Independent vs dependent vs confounding variables
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q29 · added from the options

From scratch

A variable is any characteristic that can take different values (anxiety score, dose, gender). Topic 42 defined a confound; this topic builds the full vocabulary that Q29's options were drawn from.

Independent variable (IV). The variable the experimenter manipulates (or selects) to see its effect, the presumed cause. It has at least two levels (CBT vs waitlist; 0, 5, 10 mg). A manipulated IV is set by the researcher; a subject (organismic) variable such as age, sex or diagnosis cannot be manipulated, only selected, so studies using it are quasi-experimental and cannot prove causation.

Dependent variable (DV). The measured outcome, the presumed effect, which "depends" on the IV (BDI score, reaction time, number of words recalled). Both must be operationally defined: stated in terms of the procedures used to manipulate or measure them ("anxiety = score on the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale").

Extraneous variables are any other variables that could affect the DV (room noise, time of day, participant fatigue). If they vary randomly across conditions they add noise (lower power) but do not bias. An extraneous variable becomes a confounding variable when it varies systematically with the IV and also affects the DV, so the two effects cannot be separated. Example: morning participants get the new memory training and afternoon participants get the control; time of day is confounded with treatment. Confounds threaten internal validity.

Sources of confounding: participant variables (non-random groups), situational variables, order effects in within-subjects designs (practice, fatigue; controlled by counterbalancing), demand characteristics (Martin Orne, 1962: cues that tell participants the hypothesis), experimenter expectancy effects (Robert Rosenthal: experimenters unknowingly influence results; controlled by double-blind procedures), and placebo effects (controlled by placebo groups).

In correlational research the confound is called the third-variable problem: ice-cream sales and drowning correlate because both rise with summer heat. A relationship produced entirely by a third variable is a spurious correlation.

Mediator vs moderator (Baron and Kenny, 1986). A mediator explains how or why the IV affects the DV (stress → rumination → depression; rumination is the mechanism). A moderator changes the strength or direction of the IV-DV relation (stress predicts depression more strongly in people with low social support). Tolman's intervening variable is an unobserved process linking stimulus and response (e.g., hunger, expectancy). A control variable is one deliberately held constant.

Levels of measurement (S. S. Stevens, 1946) classify variables: nominal (categories), ordinal (rank), interval (equal units, no true zero, e.g., IQ, temperature in Celsius), ratio (true zero, e.g., reaction time). Variables are also discrete (countable) or continuous.

Key distinctions

VariableRoleQ29 option it matches
IndependentManipulated, presumed cause"Intentionally manipulated"
DependentMeasured outcome"Measures the outcome"
ExtraneousAffects DV, not tied to IV"Only affects the dependent variable"
ConfoundingLinked to IV and affects DV"Influences both IV and DV" (correct)
MediatorMechanism between IV and DVNot offered
ModeratorChanges strength of IV-DV linkNot offered

MCQ traps

  • "Only affects the DV" describes an extraneous variable; a confound must co-vary with the IV too.
  • Mediator vs confound: a mediator lies on the causal path (IV causes it, it causes DV); a confound is an outside variable related to both, not caused by the IV.
  • Mediator vs moderator: "how/why" = mediator; "for whom/when" = moderator.
  • Age or gender as "IV" is a subject variable; such a study cannot claim causation even if labelled an experiment.
  • Random assignment controls confounds from participant variables, but not situational confounds like testing all treatment participants in the morning.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q29: In research, what is a confounding variable → A variable that influences both the independent and dependent variables. Every distractor is a neighbouring variable type: "intentionally manipulated" is the IV, "measures the outcome" is the DV, and "only affects the dependent variable" is a plain extraneous variable, which adds noise but does not confound because it is not linked to the IV.
82Graphs: frequency polygon, bar graph, pie chart, ogive; cumulative frequency, proportions, raw vs grouped data
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q53, Q37, Q38, Q21 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 41 named each graph. This topic shows how each is built from a worked table, the way Garrett and King and Minium teach it, because construction details are what the distractors test.

Step 1: from raw to grouped. Suppose 50 patients' scores run from 23 to 87. Range = 87 minus 23 = 64. Choose an interval width i so you get roughly 10 to 15 classes: i = 5 gives 64/5 ≈ 13 intervals (20 to 24, 25 to 29 ... 85 to 89). Start the lowest interval at a multiple of i. Each interval has stated limits (25, 29), exact limits (24.5, 29.5) and a midpoint (27). Once grouped, every score is treated as if it lay at the midpoint (the midpoint assumption), which introduces grouping error. Because i and the starting point are the researcher's choice, raw scores do not yield a unique grouped distribution, while a grouped distribution can never be turned back into the exact raw scores.

Step 2: frequency columns. Example rows (top to bottom): 40 to 44, f = 6; 35 to 39, f = 10; 30 to 34, f = 4 (N = 20). Cumulative frequency (cf) is accumulated from the bottom: 4, 14, 20; the top cf always equals N. Proportion (relative frequency) = f/N: 0.20, 0.50, 0.30, always summing to 1. Percentage = proportion × 100. Cumulative percentage = cf/N × 100: 20%, 70%, 100%. Proportions and percentages let you compare a class of 200 with a class of 50; cumulative columns tell you how many scored below an exact upper limit, which is how percentiles and the median are found.

Histogram: adjacent rectangles erected on exact limits, height = f. Frequency polygon: plot f at each midpoint, join with straight lines, and bring the line down to the baseline at the midpoints of the empty intervals just below and above the distribution, so the area under the polygon equals the area of the histogram. Polygons are preferred for comparing two or more distributions on one graph (use percentages if Ns differ). Garrett describes smoothing a polygon with a running average of three adjacent frequencies.

Ogive (cumulative frequency curve): plot cf or cumulative percentage at the upper exact limit of each interval and join with a smooth S-shaped curve. A "less than" ogive rises; a "more than" ogive falls; the two cross at the median. Reading across from the 25th or 75th cumulative percentage gives Q1 and Q3, so the ogive is the graphic route to percentiles and percentile ranks.

Bar graph: separated bars for discrete or categorical data (number of patients by diagnosis); bars may be vertical or horizontal and their order is arbitrary for nominal data. Pie chart: a circle divided into sectors, each sector's angle = proportion × 360° (25% of cases = 90°); only for parts of one whole, awkward for more than about six categories. Line graph: change over time (admissions per month). Scatterplot: two scores per person.

Key distinctions

GraphPlotted atData typeMain use
HistogramExact limits, touching barsContinuous, groupedShape of one distribution
Frequency polygonMidpoints, closed to baselineContinuous, groupedComparing distributions
OgiveUpper exact limits, cumulativeContinuousMedian, percentiles
Bar graphCategories, gapsNominal, ordinal, discreteComparing categories
Pie chartAngle = proportion × 360°Categorical parts of a wholeComposition

MCQ traps

  • Polygon at midpoints, ogive at upper exact limits. Swapping these is the commonest error.
  • Cumulative frequency stays in raw counts, so it does not solve unequal N; relative frequency (proportion or percentage) does. Cumulative percentage does both.
  • Proportion vs percentage: 0.25 vs 25%; the same information on different scales.
  • Histogram vs bar graph: touching vs separated bars; continuous vs categorical.
  • Grouping loses information; you can go from raw to grouped, never back.
  • Median from an ogive is read at the 50% point (or N/2), not at the peak.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q53: Series of rectangles, each representing one class interval → Histogram. Frequency polygon (midpoints joined by lines), bar graph (separated bars for categories) and pie chart (sectors of a circle) were the distractors, all from this topic.
  • Q37: A set of ___ scores does not result in a unique set of grouped scores → Raw; pooled, frequency and percentage were distractors, since grouping choices (i, starting point) belong to raw data.
  • Q38: Helpful when comparing distributions with different numbers of cases → Relative frequencies; cumulative frequencies was the distractor because cumulative counts are still on the raw scale; "frequency percentage" and "frequency proportion" are informal variants of the textbook term.
  • Q21: Graph of correlational data → Scatterplot; none of the single-variable graphs here can show a two-variable relationship.
83Correlational, case study, attrition vs cohort effects
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q60, Q86, Q81 · added from the options

From scratch

These were the distractor designs and threats in Q60, Q81 and Q86. Topic 42 gave the outline; here is the depth.

Correlational research measures two or more variables as they naturally occur, with no manipulation, and quantifies their relationship with a correlation coefficient ranging from minus 1 to +1. Sign gives direction; size gives strength. r² (coefficient of determination) is the proportion of variance shared: r = 0.50 means 25% shared variance. Choice of coefficient depends on data: Pearson r (both interval/ratio, linear), Spearman's rho (ranks), point-biserial (one true dichotomy, e.g., gender, one continuous), biserial (artificial dichotomy, e.g., pass/fail), phi (two true dichotomies), tetrachoric (two artificial dichotomies). Why correlation is not causation: (1) directionality problem (does depression cause poor sleep or the reverse?), (2) third-variable problem. Cross-lagged panel designs (measuring both variables at two times) give partial clues to direction. r is also distorted by restriction of range (correlating IQ and grades only among toppers lowers r), outliers and curvilinear relations (r near 0 for an inverted U). Strengths: studies variables that cannot ethically be manipulated (child abuse, smoking), allows prediction (regression).

Case study: intensive, usually long-term study of a single individual, family or unit using interviews, observation, tests and records. It is idiographic (Gordon Allport's term for study of the unique individual, vs nomothetic laws across people). Landmark cases: Anna O. (Breuer and Freud, Studies on Hysteria, 1895), Little Hans (Freud, 1909), Phineas Gage (Harlow's reports, 1848 and 1868; frontal lobe and personality), H.M. (Scoville and Milner, 1957; hippocampus and anterograde amnesia), Genie (Curtiss, 1977; critical period for language). Strengths: rare phenomena, hypothesis generation, rich detail, disconfirming a universal claim with one case. Weaknesses: no control, low generalisability, observer bias, retrospective memory, cannot establish cause. Single-case experimental designs (ABAB, multiple baseline) add experimental control to n = 1.

Developmental designs and their threats. Three effects can explain an age difference: age (maturation), cohort (generation, birth-year group) and time of measurement (historical moment of testing). K. Warner Schaie (1965), in his general developmental model, showed each simple design confounds two of these. Cross-sectional (different ages, one time) confounds age with cohort. Longitudinal (same people, many times) confounds age with time of measurement. Time-lag (same age, different years) confounds cohort with time. Sequential designs (cohort-sequential, cross-sequential) combine them.

Cohort effect example: early cross-sectional studies suggested intelligence declines from the twenties, but Schaie's Seattle Longitudinal Study showed most abilities stable until about 60. Older cohorts had less schooling. The Flynn effect (James Flynn, 1984 and 1987: IQ gains of roughly 3 points per decade across generations) is a large-scale cohort effect.

Attrition (experimental mortality, Campbell and Stanley) is dropout over time. It is a longitudinal problem: if those who drop out are sicker, poorer or less able (selective attrition), the remaining sample looks healthier and change estimates are biased. In experiments, differential attrition (more dropouts from one arm) threatens internal validity; trials use intention-to-treat analysis. Longitudinal studies also suffer practice (testing) effects, cost and investigator turnover.

Key distinctions

ThreatBelongs toMeaning
Cohort effectCross-sectionalAge groups differ by generation, not just age
AttritionLongitudinalDropouts make the remaining sample unrepresentative
Practice effectLongitudinal, within-subjectsRepeated testing improves scores
Time of measurementLongitudinalHistorical events at testing time
Third variableCorrelationalOutside variable produces the r
Low generalisabilityCase studyn = 1

MCQ traps

  • Attrition is never the answer for a cross-sectional weakness: each person is tested once, so no one can drop out over time.
  • Cohort ≠ age: a cohort is a birth group; a cohort effect is a generational difference masquerading as an age effect.
  • Case study is not an experiment, even with tests and measurements; no manipulation, no control group.
  • Strong correlation still is not causation; r = 0.90 between variables does not tell you direction.
  • Negative r is not "weak": r = minus 0.80 is stronger than r = +0.40.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q60: Design best for cause and effect → Experimental design; correlational study and case study were distractors because neither manipulates an IV or controls confounds; cross-sectional was the fourth.
  • Q86: Design best for changes over time → Longitudinal design; case study was a distractor (it can follow one person over time but gives no generalisable picture of change), alongside cross-sectional and experimental.
  • Q81: Disadvantage of cross-sectional studies → Cohort difference; participant attrition was the trap because it is a longitudinal problem; random assignment and socio-economic factors were filler.
84df formulas, effect size, power, Type I vs II errors
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q84, Q90 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 43 covered chi-square df and the p-value. This topic gives the complete df table and the decision-theory ideas (Type I/II error, power, effect size) that appeared as Q90's distractors.

Degrees of freedom = the number of values free to vary once some statistic has been fixed. If 5 scores must have a mean of 10, you may pick any 4, but the 5th is forced, so df = 4. General rule: df = number of observations minus number of parameters estimated. This is also why the sample SD divides by N minus 1 (Bessel's correction): one df is used up estimating the mean.

Testdf
One-sample tN minus 1
Paired (related) tNumber of pairs minus 1
Independent tN1 + N2 minus 2
Pearson r (testing significance)N minus 2
Chi-square goodness of fitk minus 1 (categories)
Chi-square independence(rows minus 1)(columns minus 1)
One-way ANOVABetween k minus 1; within N minus k; total N minus 1
Two-way ANOVA interaction(a minus 1)(b minus 1)
Multiple regression (residual)N minus k minus 1 (k predictors)

Type I and Type II errors (Neyman and Pearson's decision framework). A Type I error is rejecting H0 when it is true (a false positive: concluding a drug works when it does not); its probability is alpha, set in advance (0.05 or 0.01). A Type II error is failing to reject H0 when it is false (a false negative: missing a drug that does work); its probability is beta. Legal analogy: convicting an innocent person is Type I; acquitting a guilty one is Type II. Lowering alpha from .05 to .01 reduces Type I errors but increases Type II errors, other things equal. Running many tests inflates the familywise Type I error; the Bonferroni correction divides alpha by the number of tests.

Power = 1 minus beta: the probability of correctly rejecting a false H0, i.e., detecting a real effect. Power increases with (1) larger N, (2) larger effect size, (3) larger alpha, (4) one-tailed tests (in the predicted direction), (5) less error variance (reliable measures, within-subjects designs). Jacob Cohen recommended power of 0.80; his 1962 review of the Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology found the average power to detect a medium effect was below 0.5. An a priori power analysis fixes N before the study: for d = 0.5, alpha .05 two-tailed and power .80, about 64 per group are needed for an independent t test.

Effect size is the magnitude of an effect, independent of sample size. Cohen's d = (M1 minus M2) / pooled SD: 0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large. r: 0.1, 0.3, 0.5. Eta squared (ANOVA, proportion of variance explained): 0.01, 0.06, 0.14. For chi-square, phi (2 × 2) and Cramér's V; for binary clinical outcomes, odds ratio and number needed to treat. Glass's delta uses the control group SD; Hedges' g corrects d for small samples. The p-value says whether an effect is likely non-zero; effect size says how big it is.

Key distinctions

H0 trueH0 false
Reject H0Type I error (alpha)Correct: power (1 minus beta)
Retain H0Correct (1 minus alpha)Type II error (beta)

MCQ traps

  • Alpha is the Type I rate, not the p-value. Alpha is chosen before; p is computed from the data.
  • Power is 1 minus beta, not 1 minus alpha (that is the probability of correctly retaining a true H0).
  • Larger N raises power but does not change the true effect size; it shrinks p for the same effect.
  • Paired t uses pairs minus 1, not total scores minus 2.
  • Chi-square df uses rows and columns, never N.
  • A false negative (missing a real disorder) is Type II; a false alarm is Type I.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q84: df for chi-square test of independence → (N columns minus 1)(N rows minus 1). N minus 1 is the one-sample t (or goodness of fit with N categories) formula and N minus 2 the Pearson r formula, both from this df table; (columns minus 2)(rows minus 2) is invented.
  • Q90: What is the p-value → The probability of obtaining the observed data if H0 is true. Two distractors came from this topic: "the effect size of the experiment" is wrong because effect size measures magnitude, independent of N, while p depends heavily on N; "the power of the study" is wrong because power (1 minus beta) is the probability of detecting a true effect, fixed by design before data exist.
